TRYVIO (aprocitentan)

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
On December 19, 2022, Idorsia Pharmaceuticals Ltd. submitted an original NDA for
aprocitentan for the following indication: “Aprocitentan, in combination with other
antihypertensive drugs, is indicated for the treatment of hypertension, to lower blood pressure in
patients who are not adequately controlled on other drugs.”
Aprocitentan is an endothelin receptor antagonist (ERA) that inhibits the binding of endothelin
(ET)-1 to ETA and ETB receptors. ET-1, via its receptors (ETA and ETB), mediates a variety of
deleterious effects such as vasoconstriction, fibrosis, cell proliferation, and inflammation. ET-1
has been implicated in the pathogenesis of hypertension, including mediation of endothelial
dysfunction, vascular hypertrophy and remodeling, sympathetic activation, and increased
aldosterone synthesis. Of note, aprocitentan has the same chemical structure as the active
metabolite of macitentan, which is approved for the treatment of pulmonary arterial
hypertension.
In support of the proposed indication, the Applicant submitted the results of an adequate and
well-controlled multipart, Phase 3 study (PRECISION) in adults with a systolic blood
pressure ≥140 mmHg who were prescribed at least three antihypertensive medications. The
study included a 4-week double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period in which 730 subjects
were randomized 1:1:1 to aprocitentan 12.5 mg, 25 mg, or placebo once daily (Part 1), a 32-week
period in which all subjects were treated with aprocitentan 25 mg (Part 2), and another doubleblind period in which subjects were rerandomized to receive either aprocitentan 25 mg or
placebo (randomized withdrawal/Part 3).
The review team has concluded that the application provides substantial evidence of
aprocitentan’s effectiveness in reducing blood pressure when used in combination with other
antihypertensive drugs in subjects who are not adequately controlled on other drugs. Numerous
antihypertensive drugs, from a variety of pharmacologic classes and with different mechanisms
of action, have been shown in randomized controlled trials to reduce cardiovascular morbidity
and mortality. Hence, the Agency accepts blood pressure lowering as a validated surrogate
endpoint and basis for traditional approval of antihypertensive agents. The product’s efficacy in
lowering blood pressure was demonstrated in both the initial double-blind treatment period as
well as in the randomized withdrawal period of the Phase 3 study. As such, the study is
considered to be the scientific equivalent of two clinical investigations.
As previously noted, aprocitentan has the same chemical structure as the active metabolite of
macitentan and the results of safety analyses were as a whole consistent with risks that might be
expected given the known risks of macitentan and other endothelin receptor antagonists. Because
of the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity, aprocitentan will only be available through a restricted
distribution program under a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS).
The Applicant has proposed use as an add-on therapy in patients with uncontrolled hypertension
and given the product’s safety profile, aprocitentan will not be approved as initial (first-line)

Dimension

Evidence and Uncertainties
 Anemia is a known risk of endothelin receptor antagonists and is
thought to be due in part to hemodilution. In the initial 4-week doubleblind treatment period, the incidence of a hemoglobin decrease
>2 g/dL compared to baseline was greater in subjects receiving
aprocitentan (7% and 3% in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg
arms, respectively) compared to subjects treated with placebo (1%).
Hemoglobin decreases usually presented early, stabilized thereafter,
and were reversible after discontinuation.
 Fluid retention is a known risk of endothelin receptor antagonists. In
Part 1, fluid retention was reported in 9% of subjects receiving
aprocitentan 12.5 mg as compared with 18% of subjects receiving
aprocitentan 25 mg and 2% on placebo. The majority of edema
events were reported as mild to moderate in severity and did not lead
to treatment discontinuation.
 Endothelin receptor antagonists can cause hepatotoxicity, though the
degree of risk varies amongst members of the class. In PRECISION,
there were two subjects with liver enzyme elevations of greater than
5-fold the upper limit of normal which were assessed as highly likely
related to the use of aprocitentan. Both subjects had positive
rechallenges. There were no cases of ALT and/or AST >3 × ULN and
total bilirubin >2 × ULN or liver failure.
• Based on data from animal reproduction studies with endothelin receptor
antagonists, aprocitentan can cause fetal harm when administered during
pregnancy.
• Based on data from other endothelin receptor anatagonists, aprocitetan
may have an adverse effect on spermatogenesis.
• Hypersensitivity related-AEs were reported in 0.8% of subjects taking
aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 0.4% of subjects taking 25 mg compared with
no patients receiving placebo in Part. During Part 2, one subject taking
25 mg had an SAE of allergic dermatitis requiring hospitalization and
experienced recurrence of the allergic dermatitis with rechallenge.

The primary objective of the PRECISION study was to demonstrate the blood pressure-lowering effect of aprocitentan when added to standard-of-care in subjects with “true” resistant
hypertension. The substantial reduction in blood pressure seen in the placebo arm in Part 1 of the study indicates that while the study population had uncontrolled hypertension, it was
not necessarily “true” resistant hypertension.

TRYVIO (aprocitentan)

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Aprocitentan is an orally active, dual endothelin-receptor antagonist (ERA) that inhibits the
binding of endothelin (ET)-1 to ET receptors A and B (ETA and ETB). ET-1, via its receptors
(ETA and ETB), mediates a variety of deleterious effects such as vasoconstriction, fibrosis, cell
proliferation and inflammation. ET-1 upregulation and can cause endothelial dysfunction,
vascular hypertrophy and remodeling, sympathetic activation and increased aldosterone
synthesis, and thus, contributes to blood pressure elevation. Aprocitentan has the same chemical
structure as the active metabolite of macitentan. Macitentan is approved for the treatment of
pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH, WHO Group I) to reduce the risks of disease progression
and hospitalization for PAH.
On December 19, 2022, the Applicant submitted an NDA for aprocitentan for the treatment of
hypertension to lower blood pressure under section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and
Cosmetic Act.
Disease Background
The prevalence of hypertension in the United States is high,2 and nearly half of adults in the
United States (approximately 48%) have a systolic blood pressure >130 mmHg, diastolic blood
pressure >80 mmHg, or are taking a medication for hypertension
(Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2023). In the United States, the prevalence of
hypertension is higher among males, older adults, Black adults, and those with obesity, chronic
kidney disease, or diabetes (Ostchega et al. 2020). Hypertension is associated with an increased
risk of cardiovascular disease, stroke, and end-stage kidney disease. In 2010, in the United
States, hypertension accounted for more cardiovascular deaths than any other modifiable
cardiovascular disease risk factor and was second to cigarette smoking as a preventable cause of
death for any reason (Whelton et al. 2018). A continuous association exists between higher blood
pressure and increased cardiovascular disease risk, and even modest reductions of severe
hypertension can provide substantial benefit.
Treatment of hypertension is among the most common reasons for healthcare office visits and for
the use of chronic prescription medications. Control of high blood pressure with medication is
part of comprehensive cardiovascular risk management (e.g., lipid control, smoking cessation,
exercise, and limited sodium intake). Numerous antihypertensive drugs, from a variety of
pharmacologic classes and with different mechanisms of action are available and have been
shown in randomized controlled trials to reduce cardiovascular morbidity and mortality. From
these trials, it can be concluded that it is the blood pressure reduction, and not some other
pharmacologic property of the drugs, that is largely responsible for those benefits. The largest
2 The exact prevalence of hypertension depends upon the definition of hypertension and varies according to the

guideline/threshold used. Per the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) conducted between
2017-2018, in the United States, approximately 45% of U.S. adults met the definition of hypertension per the 2017
ACC/AHA guideline (i.e., BP >130/80 mmHg).

10
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
and most consistent cardiovascular outcome benefit has been a reduction in the risk of stroke, but
reductions in myocardial infarction and cardiovascular mortality also have been seen regularly.
Many patients will require more than one drug from different pharmacological classes to achieve
blood pressure goals. Primary agents for the treatment of high blood pressure include thiazide
diuretics, angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs), and
calcium channel blockers. Secondary agents include loop diuretics, potassium-sparing diuretics,
aldosterone antagonists, beta-blockers, direct renin inhibitors, alpha-1 blockers, and centrally
acting drugs, and direct vasodilators (Whelton et al. 2018).
Hypertension that is difficult-to-control is often characterized as “uncontrolled” (i.e., blood
pressure above goal) or “resistant” hypertension. Resistant hypertension is defined as blood
pressure that remains above goal despite concurrent use of three antihypertensive agents of
different classes at maximum recommended (or maximally tolerated) doses. If tolerated, one of
the three agents should be a diuretic. Patients whose blood pressure is controlled with four or
more medications are also considered to have resistant hypertension. “True” resistant
hypertension is distinguished from “apparent resistant” and pseudoresistant hypertension through
24-hour ambulatory blood pressure monitoring and lack of attribution to other factors (e.g., poor
adherence to antihypertensive therapy, white coat hypertension, poor adherence to dietary and
lifestyle approaches, suboptimal antihypertensive therapy, and inaccurate measurement of blood
pressure). The prevalence of true resistant hypertension is not known, and many patients who
meet the definition of resistant hypertension have been found to have poor adherence or
inadequate treatment regimens. Data from the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey
from 2003 through 2008 in nonpregnant US adults with hypertension showed that approximately
9% met criteria for apparent resistant hypertension. Adults with resistant hypertension were
more likely to be older, non-Hispanic black, have higher body mass index, albuminuria, reduced
renal function, and self-reported medical histories of coronary heart disease, heart failure, stroke,
and diabetes mellitus (Persell 2011).

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
5.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
ET-1 mediates a variety of deleterious effects via activation of its receptors (ETA and ETB), such
as vasoconstriction, fibrosis, proliferation, hypertrophy, and inflammation. Aprocitentan is an
ERA that potently inhibits the binding of ET-1 to ETA and ETB receptors. Aprocitentan showed
receptor inhibitor behavior both in vitro and in vivo, with a consistent preferential inhibition for
ETA versus ETB receptors noted across studies.

14
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
•

The inhibitory effect of aprocitentan on receptor function was assessed using microsomal
membranes from Chinese hamster ovary (CHO)-K1 cells expressing recombinant human
ETA or ETB receptors. In this assay, aprocitentan inhibited 125I-ET-1 binding to human
recombinant ETA and ETB receptors with half maximal inhibitory concentration values of
3.4±0.4 nM and 987±185 nM, respectively. Therefore, inhibition of ETA was approximately
290-fold more potent than for ETB in this cell-free study.

•

In recombinant cells, aprocitentan inhibited the ET-1-induced increase in cytosolic Ca2+ (a
measure of downstream ET-1 signaling) with a geometric mean Schild Kb inhibitor potency
value of 5.5 nM and 319 nM on ETA and ETB receptors, respectively, where Kb is the
inhibition constant of the binding of the antagonist to the receptor. Thus, inhibition was
approximately 60-fold more potent for ETA than ETB in intact cells.

•

Aprocitentan inhibited ET-1-induced contraction (ETA-mediated) in endothelium denuded rat
aorta and sarafotoxic S6c-induced contraction (ETB-mediated) in epithelium denuded rat
trachea with pA2 values of 6.7±0.2 and 5.5±0.3, respectively. The pA2 value represents
negative logarithm of the molar concentration of an antagonist that produces a 2-fold shift in
the concentration response curve. These data demonstrated functional inhibitory activity of
aprocitentan on ETA or ETB in isolated tissues.

•

Given the difference in potency between ETA and ETB observed in vitro, a study was
conducted to establish the inhibitory effect of aprocitentan on the ETB receptor in vivo,
which is reported to be involved in the clearance of ET-1 from the plasma (Loffler et al.
1993; Fukuroda et al. 1994).(Loffler et al. 1993; Fukuroda et al. 1994). Aprocitentan
treatment in normotensive rats resulted in a dose-dependent increase in plasma ET-1
demonstrating its inhibitory effect on ETB in vivo. These data indicate that while a greater
potency for ETA versus ETB exists, as demonstrated in vitro, aprocitentan can also inhibit the
ETB receptor in vivo.

5.1.2. Animal Model Data Showing Proof of Concept
for Efficacy
Proof of concept supporting the efficacy of aprocitentan for the proposed indication was
demonstrated in various animal models including deoxycorticosterone acetate (DOCA)-salt rats
in which hypertension is induced by unilateral nephrectomy, mineralocorticoid analog DOCA
pellet implantation and exposure to dietary salt (1% NaCl in drinking water); Dahl-S rats in
which hypertension is induced by increased dietary salt (1% NaCl in drinking water);
spontaneously hypertensive (SHR) rats in which hypertension is mediated through the renin
angiotensin system and sympathetic nervous system.
Acute administration of aprocitentan resulted in a dose-dependent decrease in blood pressure
(BP) without any effect on heart rate in all three animal models, demonstrating an
antihypertensive effect. At a dose of 100 mg/kg, aprocitentan decreased mean arterial pressure
more effectively in Dahl-S (↓36 mmHg) and DOCA-salt (↓29 mmHg) rats than in SHR (↓18
mmHg), indicating that activation of the ET system was more prominent in salt-sensitive and
volume-dependent hypertensive models (DOCA-salt and Dahl-S models) compared to the
essential hypertension model (SHR rats).
15
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
The protective effect of aprocitentan on heart and kidney function was evaluated in DOCA-salt
rats following a 4-week treatment period. In DOCA-salt rats, aprocitentan dose-dependently
attenuated mean arterial pressure (without affecting heart rate), increased renal blood flow,
decreased renal vascular resistance, and reduced elevated plasma N-terminal pro-brain natriuretic
peptide (NT-proBNP) levels. However, treatment did not fully ameliorate the cardiac
(cardiomyopathic condition) and kidney (minimal to marked hydronephrosis, tubulonephrosis,
interstitial nephritis and glomerular changes) findings. The results of this study suggest that
aprocitentan treatment may partially improve cardiac and renal function in a hypertensive rat
model.
Treatment with aprocitentan in combination with other standard antihypertensive therapies was
also evaluated in DOCA-salt rats and SHR rats. Aprocitentan, compared with spironolactone,
was tested in combination with other standard antihypertensive therapies (e.g., valsartan,
enalapril and amlodipine). The aprocitentan/valsartan combination demonstrated synergistic
effect in DOCA-salt and SHR rats whereas the spironolactone/valsartan combination only
produced additive effects in these models. The aprocitentan/enalapril combination demonstrated
synergistic effect in SHR rats and an additive effect in DOCA-salt rats. The
aprocitentan/amlodipine combination showed only additive effects in both rat models. These
results suggested that aprocitentan treatment may have an additive or synergistic effect on BP
reduction when coadministered with other antihypertensive drugs.

5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
•

No dose adjustment is required in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment.
Aprocitentan has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Section 8.1).

•

No dose adjustment is needed for patients with renal impairment; however, aprocitentan was
not studied in patients with eGFR <15 mL/min or in patients undergoing dialysis and is not
recommended in these patients (Section 8.1).

•

Aprocitentan did not alter the pharmacokinetic (PK) profile of midazolam or 1-hydroxy
midazolam to a clinically relevant extent (Section 8.2).

•

Aprocitentan did not alter the PK of rosuvastatin to a clinically relevant extent (Section 8.2).

•

Aprocitentan may be taken with or without food (Section 8.2).

•

Glucosidation of aprocitentan to M3 accounts for 25.2% of the administered dose
(Section 8.2).

Distribution
Volume of distribution
19.3 L
Plasma protein binding 99.5% (in vitro)
Drug as substrate of
Aprocitentan is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP in vitro. Inhibitors of these
transporters
transporters are not expected to influence the PK of aprocitentan. Aprocitentan
has a high permeability across cell membranes, so the cellular uptake is mainly
driven by passive diffusion.
Elimination
Mass balance results
Urine was the primary route of elimination. A total of 52% of drug material was
eliminated in the urine, and 25% was eliminated in feces. In the plasma, no
drug metabolite was detected that exceeded 10% of drug-related exposure.
The main metabolites in urine were M3, M6, M16, and A1. The main products
in feces were M1 and unchanged aprocitentan. Two metabolic pathways
contributed to >25% of 14C-radioactive elimination–glucosidation of
aprocitentan to M3, and hydrolysis of aprocitentan to M1, which then
undergoes other reactions leading to A1, A4, A5, M4, M5, M6, M11, M13, and
M16.
Clearance
CL/F is approximately 0.3 L/h
Half-life
Terminal plasma half-life is approximately 46 hours, and effective half-life is
approximately 41 hours
Metabolic pathway(s)
In the mass balance study, two metabolic pathways contributing to >25% of
14C-radioactive elimination were detected:
• Glucosidation of aprocitentan to M3 by UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 (accounts for
approximately 25.2% of the administered dose)
Hydrolysis of aprocitentan to M1 which then undergoes several other reactions
leading to A1, A4, A5, M4, M5, M6, M11, and M13 (accounts for approximately
31.5% of the administered dose). M1 is formed by both chemical and
enzymatic hydrolysis. It is believed that the hydrolysis is mostly chemical rather
than enzymatic.
Primary excretion
After a single oral dose of radiolabeled aprocitentan, approximately 52% of the
pathways (% dose)
dose was eliminated via urine (0.2% unchanged) and 25% (6.8% unchanged)
via feces.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight
Based on popPK analysis, body weight does not affect PK to a clinically
relevant extent (Section 14.5.2.5).
Age
Healthy elderly subjects (aged 65 to 80 years) Cmax: 1.3-fold and AUCτ: 1.2-fold
of that of healthy nonelderly subjects (aged 18 to 45 years)
Renal impairment
After a 50 mg dose of aprocitentan, compared to healthy subjects, the
geometric mean ratio for Cmax was 1.04 in those with severe renal impairment.
The AUC0­t and AUC0­∞ were 32% and 34% higher, respectively, in those with
severe renal impairment compared to those with normal renal function.
Hepatic impairment
After a 25 mg dose of aprocitentan, compared to healthy subjects, the
geometric mean ratios for Cmax, AUC0­t, and AUC0­∞ were 1.03, 1.2, and 1.2,
respectively, in those with moderate hepatic impairment.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
the results of the Phase 2 dose-finding study and Phase 1 pharmacodynamic study (Study AC080-102), which suggested that doses of aprocitentan in the range of 10 mg to 25 mg might
provide the best benefit/risk profile. The phase 3 study also evaluated the 25 mg dose in a
randomized withdrawal period.
Data Supporting Dose Selection for the Applicant’s Phase 3 Study
In the Phase 2 dose-finding study, subjects with essential hypertension were randomized to
receive aprocitentan (5, 10, 25, or 50 mg), 20 mg lisinopril, or placebo for 8 weeks. Aprocitentan
doses of 10, 25, and 50 mg decreased sitting systolic/diastolic unattended automated office blood
pressure measurement (uAOBPM) from baseline to Week 8, with placebo-corrected reductions
of 7.1/4.9, 9.9/7.0, and 7.6/5.0 mmHg, respectively, compared with a reduction of 4.8/3.8 mmHg
with lisinopril 20 mg. For those with valid ambulatory blood pressure monitor readings,
aprocitentan 10, 25, and 50 mg significantly decreased placebo-corrected 24-hour
systolic/diastolic blood pressure by 4.0/4.0, 4.8/5.9, and 3.7/4.5 mmHg, respectively. Based on
the Phase 2 study results, the Applicant noted that the maximum effect on blood pressure
appeared to correspond to the 25 mg dose, with similar effects on both uAOBPM and ambulatory
blood pressure monitoring (ABPM) readings at the 10 mg and 50 mg doses. For additional
discussion of the Phase 2 study results, see Section 14.2.
The Applicant also conducted a Phase 1 study to better understand the relationship between the
dose of aprocitentan and risk of fluid retention (as assessed by changes in body weight). In this
Phase 1 study, healthy male subjects on a high sodium diet were randomized to placebo or
aprocitentan doses of 10, 25, or 50 mg, and to one of two treatment sequences (A-B or B-A).
Treatment A received aprocitentan once daily from Day 1 to Day 9, and Treatment B received
placebo from Day 1 to Day 9. The 10 mg dose was considered to be “non-inferior” to placebo
because the upper limit of the 90% confidence interval (CI) of body weight increase did not
exceed 1 kg (mean increase of +0.4 kg, CI 0.05-0.8). In contrast, the 25 mg and 50 mg doses had
an upper limit of the 90% CI that exceeded 1 kg (mean body weight increases of +0.8 kg,
CI 0.03-1.5 and +0.8 kg, CI 0.3-1.3, respectively), suggesting a greater risk at the 25 mg as
compared to the 10 mg dose. For additional discussion of the results of Study AC-080-102, see
Section 14.2.
Findings in Applicant’s Phase 3 Study
As discussed in Section 6.2, the Applicant’s Phase 3 study was a multipart study in adults with a
systolic blood pressure (SBP) ≥140 mmHg (based on uAOBPM) who were prescribed at least
three antihypertensive medications. In Part 1 of the study, 730 subjects were randomized 1:1:1 to
aprocitentan 12.5 mg, aprocitentan 25 mg, or placebo once daily for a 4-week double-blind
treatment period. The mean (+ SE) change in SBP at 4 weeks was -15.3±0.9 mmHg for
aprocitentan 12.5 mg, -15.2±0.9 mmHg for 25 mg, and -11.5±0.9 mmHg for placebo, for a
difference versus placebo of -3.8±1.3 mmHg (97.5% CI: -6.8, -0.8; p =0.004) and -3.7±1.3
mmHg (97.5% CI: -6.7, -0.8; p=0.005), respectively. The results of exploratory analyses based
on other blood pressure parameters (e.g., mean sitting diastolic blood pressure [SiDBP])
measured by automated office blood pressure measurement (AOBPM), 24 hour mean SBP
measured by ABPM, 24 hour mean diastolic blood pressure (DBP) measured by ABPM, daytime
mean SBP measured by ABPM, daytime mean DBP measured by ABPM, night-time mean SBP

measured by ABPM, and night-time mean DBP measured by ABPM) are shown in Table 6.
Based on the Applicant’s point estimates, the difference in BP response, if real, is modest.

TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
standard background antihypertensive therapy (SBAT) consisting of an ARB (valsartan,
160 mg), a calcium channel blocker (amlodipine, 5 or 10 mg), and a diuretic
(hydrochlorothiazide, 25 mg). SBAT was continued throughout the study. Subjects with
concomitant use of a stable dose of a beta blocker (any indication) that was initiated at least 4
weeks prior to the screening visit continued this treatment throughout the study.
Following the 4-week placebo run-in period, 730 subjects (whose SiSBP (uAOBPM) remained
≥140 mmHg) were randomized 1:1:1 to aprocitentan 12.5 mg, aprocitentan 25 mg, or placebo
once daily for the initial 4-week double-blind (DB) treatment period (Part 1). At the end of 4
weeks, all subjects entered the single-blind treatment period (Part 2) where they received
aprocitentan 25 mg once daily for 32 weeks. At the end of the 32 weeks, subjects were
rerandomized to receive either aprocitentan 25 mg or placebo, once daily, during a 12-week DBwithdrawal (WD) period (Part 3) (Figure 2). All subjects then entered a 30-day safety follow-up
period.
Figure 2. PRECISION Study Design

Source: PRECISION clinical study report
Abbreviations: EOS, end of study; EOT, end of treatment; FU, follow-up; V, visit

Objectives
The primary objective was to demonstrate the BP-lowering effect of aprocitentan when added to
standard-of-care in subjects with true resistant hypertension3 (RHT).
The secondary objectives were: (1) to demonstrate that the effect of aprocitentan on BP is
durable when added to standard-of-care in subjects with true RHT, and (2) to evaluate the longterm safety and tolerability of aprocitentan in subjects with true RHT during 48 weeks of
treatment.

3 Resistant hypertension was defined as subjects who failed to achieve their recommended BP treatment target with

maximum tolerated doses of an appropriate regimen of 3 antihypertensive drugs from different classes. Subjects
with pseudoresistant hypertension were to be excluded from the study (e.g., inappropriate blood pressure
measurement, white coat effect, insufficient efforts to optimize therapy, or poor adherence).

23
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline to Week 4 of double-blind
treatment in mean trough SiSBP, recorded via uAOBPM. Baseline was defined as the last
available measurement before the start of DB treatment.
The key secondary efficacy endpoint was the change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week
40 in mean trough SiSBP, recorded via uAOBPM.
Unattended Automated Office Blood Pressure Measurements
The blood pressure procedures used for assessments followed the American Heart Association
guidelines/Canadian Education Program on Hypertension (Pickering et al. 2005; Daskalopoulou
et al. 2012). Unattended automated office blood pressure was measured throughout the study
with an automatic oscillometric sphygmomanometer which was provided to each site by the
central blood pressure laboratory. At each study visit, blood pressure was measured
noninvasively from the same arm by the provided AOBPM device, which recorded multiple
sitting blood pressure readings with the subject resting undisturbed and unattended in a quiet
place for five minutes. At each assessment, SiSBP and SiDBP were measured five times with a
1-minute time interval between them. After discarding the first reading, the blood pressure
device/system generated the average of the remaining four readings for SiSBP and SiDBP. Blood
pressure was measured at trough (i.e., before taking the study treatment and the standardized
background antihypertensive therapy) at around the same clock time.
The baseline uAOBPM was defined as the last available measurement before or on the day of
randomization. The DB-WD baseline (Week 36) was defined as the last available measurement
before or on the day of rerandomization.
For additional details, including procedures for assessing other blood pressure parameters, see
Section 15.

6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, PRECISION Study

Analysis Sets
Full Analysis Set (FAS): All subjects who were randomized and have a baseline SiSBP recorded
via uAOBPM.
Modified FAS: All subjects from the FAS who were rerandomized in the DB-WD part of the
study and who have a DB-WD baseline SiSBP recorded via uAOBPM.
Sample Size Calculation
The Applicant planned the sample size based on the secondary endpoint to maintain at least 85%
power for the DB-WD period (Part 3). At least 600 subjects were to be randomized and at least
380 subjects were expected to be re‑randomized in the DB‑WD period.
Specifically, a sample size of 380 would provide 90% power to detect difference of 5 mmHg
between aprocitentan 25 mg and placebo at two-sided alpha of 0.05, assuming the within-group
SD for the change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough SiSBP as
measured by uAOBPM was around 15 mmHg (based on Study AC-080A201).

27
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Primary Analysis
The primary analysis was conducted on the FAS for the three treatment groups (aprocitentan
25 mg, aprocitentan 12.5 mg, and placebo). The primary endpoint, change from baseline to
Week 4 (Part 1) in mean trough SiSBP measured by uAOBPM, was analyzed using a mixedeffects model for repeated measures (MMRM) analysis with factors for treatment group, visit
and treatment by visit interaction, and covariates for baseline SiSBP and the interaction between
baseline SiSBP and visit. An unstructured covariance matrix was used to account for the within
subject correlation.
Intercurrent Event/Missing Data Handling
A treatment policy strategy was used to handle intercurrent events including “Premature
discontinuation of DB treatment,” “the addition / dose increase of a diuretic,” and “use of rescue
medication” in the primary and secondary analyses (i.e., all uAOBPM measurements,
irrespective of any intercurrent event(s), were to be included in the analysis).
Missing data were not to be imputed but were to be handled by a mixed model based on the
assumption that the data were missing at random (MAR).
Sensitivity Analyses
The impact of deviations from the MAR assumption was to be assessed by the following
sensitivity analyses:
•

Copy reference multiple imputation

•

Jump to reference multiple imputation

•

Tipping point analysis

Alternative estimand for treatment discontinuation and rescue medication:
•

Hypothetical strategy (i.e., uAOBPM recordings obtained more than 1 day after premature
discontinuation of double-blind treatment were excluded from the main analysis (i.e.,
considered missing). uAOBPM recordings obtained after the addition of a diuretic, or use of
rescue medication, were also excluded from the analysis.)

Additional sensitivity analysis:
•

The primary analysis was to be performed excluding site 4204 given data integrity considers
at the site (as per internal audit report).

Subgroup Analyses
The following subgroups were prespecified:
•

Age at screening (years): 18 to <65, 65 to <75, and ≥75

•

Sex: male, female

•

Weight at baseline (kg): ≤100, >100

•

Race: Black or African American, White, Asian, Other
 ‘Other’ includes American Indian or Alaska Native, Native Hawaiian or other Pacific
Islander, and Other. The ‘Other’ category was not considered for subgroup analyses due
to the mix of races and small number of subjects limiting the ability (i.e., low power) to

Region: Asia/Australia, Europe, North America

Protocol Version 1/2 versus protocol Version 3 at randomization: compared to the prior versions
of the protocol, in protocol Version 3 Amendment 2, diuretics were no longer required to be one
of the three antihypertensive classes for enrollment in the study.
Subgroups were to be analyzed using the same model as the primary analysis. Interaction pvalues were obtained from adding subgroup by treatment and subgroup by treatment by visit
interaction terms to the primary analysis model.
Secondary Analysis
The secondary analysis (at randomized withdrawal phase) was to be conducted on the modified
FAS for the two treatment groups (aprocitentan 25 mg and placebo). The secondary endpoint,
change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough SiSBP recorded via
uAOBPM, was analyzed using a mixed model with factors for stratum (i.e., randomized
treatment in Part 1), treatment group, visit, and treatment by visit interaction, and covariates for
DB-WD baseline SiSBP and the interaction between DB-WD baseline SiSBP and visit, and an
unstructured covariance matrix.
Similar sensitivity analyses as for the primary analysis were to be conducted.
Testing Strategy
Three null hypotheses were to be tested in the study. The first two were based on the primary
endpoint in DB Part 1, the third hypothesis was based on the secondary endpoint at DB-WD Part
3 (Figure 3).
•

First null hypothesis (H10): there was no difference between aprocitentan 25 mg and placebo
in the mean change from baseline to Week 4 in mean trough SiSBP in Part 1.

•

Second null hypothesis (H20): there was no difference between aprocitentan 12.5 mg and
placebo in the mean change from baseline to Week 4 in mean trough SiSBP in Part 1.

29
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
•

Third null hypothesis (H30): there was no difference between aprocitentan 25 mg and
placebo in the mean change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough
SiSBP in Part 3.

The first two (H10 and H20) were tested in parallel using the Bonferroni correction (each with a
two-sided alpha of 0.025), and the third (H30) was only to be tested if H10 or H20 was rejected.
Figure 3. Testing Strategy

Source: Applicant’s statistical analysis plan
Abbreviations: α, level of significance; H10, first null hypothesis; H20, second null hypothesis; H30, third null hypothesis; µ, population
mean; V, mean changes from baseline for each endpoint

Statistical Analysis Plan Versions
The study was conducted from June 18, 2018 (first subject, first visit) to April 25, 2022 (last
subject, last visit). Version 1 of the statistical analysis plan (SAP) was finalized on April 4, 2019.
The SAP was subsequently amended twice. Version 2 was finalized on September 23, 2021. In
this amendment, the details of the reference based multiple imputation were added. Version 3 of
the SAP (the latest version) was finalized on April 6, 2022. In this amendment, the primary
estimand was changed from using a hypothetical strategy to a treatment policy strategy based on
the Agency’s feedback.
The changes that were made to the SAP via these amendments do not raise concerns about the
interpretability of the study results.

by a discussion of Part 1 and Part 3.

the course of the three parts of the study, the main reasons for study treatment discontinuation

Results of Part 1
Demographic
Baseline demographics in Part 1 were balanced among the treatment arms (Table 8). The median
age was 63 years (range 24 to 84 years). At baseline, 44% of subjects were aged ≥65 years.
Approximately 60% of subjects were male. The majority of subjects were white (83%); 11%
were Black or African American, and 5% were Asian. Approximately 10% were Hispanic or
Latino (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2023).8 The study was an international study
and of those enrolled, approximately 32% were enrolled at sites in North America. Of those
enrolled at U.S. sites, 37% were Black or African American.

8 Per the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) conducted between 2017-2018, in the

United States, approximately 51% of men, 75% of those ≥60 years old, 57% of non-Hispanic Black adults, 44% of
non-Hispanic white adults, and 44% of Hispanic adults met the definition of hypertension as defined by the 2017
ACC/AHA guideline (i.e., BP >130/80 mmHg).

Disposition
Among the 730 randomized subjects, 26 (3.6%) discontinued treatment and 8 (1.1%)
discontinued the study during Part 1 (Table 10); a total of 704 (96.4%) subjects in Part 1 entered

common reason for study drug discontinuation in the aprocitentan groups was adverse event.
There was 1 death during Part 1 in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg group. For further information, see
Section 7.6.2.

Analyses of Primary Endpoint
The primary endpoint of change from baseline in SiSBP at Week 4 was analyzed using an
MMRM model. Because of concerns with the Applicant’s prespecified analytic approach
(i.e., that the approach to handling missing data was not reasonable and was in violation of the
intent-to-treat [ITT] principle), the review team used a different method to analyze the primary
endpoint (see “Missing data handling and FDA recommended primary analysis” below for
further discussion of this issue). Based on the primary analysis recommended by FDA, statistical
significance was demonstrated at a two-sided alpha level of 0.025 for aprocitentan 12.5 mg
versus placebo [least square mean (LSM) difference: -3.8 mmHg; 97.5% CI:-6.8, -0.8; pvalue=0.0043], and for aprocitentan 25 mg versus placebo [LSM difference: -3.7 mmHg; 97.5%
CI:-6.6, -0.7; p-value=0.0054] (Table 11). The results are similar to the Applicant’s prespecified
34
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
analysis. See Section 6.3.1 for a discussion of the clinical meaningfulness of the treatment effect
for the two doses of aprocitentan evaluated.
There was no planned statistical comparison of the two aprocitentan doses, but point estimates
and confidence intervals for treatment effects were numerically similar for the 12.5 mg (low)
dose and 25 mg (high) dose of aprocitentan.
See Sections 6.1 and 6.3 for additional analyses and discussion regarding treatment effects on
blood pressure for the 12.5 mg and 25 mg doses of aprocitentan.

Missing Data Handling and FDA Recommended Primary Analysis
Missing SiSBP data at baseline, Week 2 and Week 4 are shown in Table 12 by treatment arm.
No subjects had missing baseline values. In the overall study population, there were moderate
missing rates for uAOBPM measurements at Week 2 and Week 4 (10% and 7%, respectively). A
total of 20 (3%) subjects were missing data at both Week 2 and Week 4.

There are two concerns with how the prespecified primary analysis handled these missing data:
1) Since the missing data were handled directly by MMRM, the 20 (3%) subjects missing both
Week 2 and Week 4 data were not included in the primary analyses given that no
postbaseline data were collected. This violates the ITT principle and could potentially
introduce bias even though the impact might be small given the low missing rate (3%).
2) For subjects that were included in the MMRM model, all the missing data were imputed
under the MAR assumption, which is not the approach recommended by the FDA. During
product development, FDA had indicated to the Applicant that “MMRM assumes the data
missing at random (MAR). Subjects who receive rescue medication, prematurely discontinue
treatment or withdraw from the study are likely to be different from those who remain in the
study on treatment.”
To further explore and address the above missing data issues, subjects in the FAS were divided
into four groups based on their missing data status after treatment discontinuation:
•

Group a: Subjects who completed study treatment through Part 1 and completed study in Part
1 with available primary endpoint data.

•

Group b: Subjects who did not complete study treatment through Part 1 but completed study
in Part 1 with available primary endpoint data, i.e., retrieved dropouts.

•

Group c: Subjects who completed study treatment through Part 1, but with missing primary
endpoint data.

•

Group d: Others, i.e., subjects who did not complete study treatment through Part 1 and with
missing primary endpoint data, or subjects who discontinued study in Part 1.

A summary for subjects by their missing data status are shown in Table 13.

In principle, a retrieved dropout multiple imputation is preferred to handle missing data due to
loss of follow-up and study withdrawal. However, according to Table 13, there were not enough
retrieved dropout subjects to support a reliable retrieved dropout multiple imputation (Table 13).
After further consideration, the statistical team concluded that missing data should be handled in
the primary analysis using a mixture of jump to reference multiple imputation based on MNAR
and standard multiple imputation based on MAR. Specifically, under the treatment policy
strategy to handle intercurrent events as specified in the SAP, missing primary endpoint data for
subjects in group 1c were imputed based on subjects in group 1a with standard multiple
imputation (MAR assumption) and missing data in group 1d were imputed with jump to
reference multiple imputation. This approach follows the ITT principle by including all
randomized subjects and handles missing data more appropriately when the missing data
assumptions are unverifiable. Similar results were obtained as compared to the prespecified
primary analysis.
Sensitivity Analyses
To further assess the robustness of the primary analysis results to missing data imputation
assumptions, the scientific misconduct reported at study Site 4204, and intercurrent event
handling, the following sensitivity analyses were conducted.
•

For the reference-based multiple imputation approaches, copy reference and jump to
reference multiple imputations were performed respectively. As opposed to the primary
analysis, all subjects were included in the MMRM.

•

For Site 4204, scientific misconduct, which led to subject participation in 2 clinical studies
concurrently and serious violation of ALCOA+ (Principles of data integrity: attributable,
legible, contemporaneous, original, accurate, complete, consistent, enduring, available)
requirements for study site study documentation was identified. Therefore, a sensitivity
analysis was conducted excluding all 27 randomized subjects from Site 4204.

•

Since the primary analysis used a treatment policy strategy for treatment discontinuation and
rescue medication, a supplementary analysis was performed using a hypothetical strategy for
rescue medication, in which any data after the intercurrent event of rescue medication were
ignored and then imputed using jump to reference multiple imputation.

37
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Results of the sensitivity/supplementary analyses showed similar treatment effects as compared
to the primary analyses, and the p-values are all smaller than 0.025, indicating that the primary
analyses results are robust (Table 14).

Subgroup Analyses
Subgroups analyses were conducted using the same model as used in the primary analyses.
Treatment effects were consistent across prespecified subgroups for both dose arms, except for
the UACR and CKD subgroups, for which interaction p-values indicated potential heterogeneity
(interaction p-value of 0.0011 and 0.0019, respectively) (Figure 4 and Figure 5).
For baseline UACR categories (<30 mg/g, 30-300 mg/g, >300 mg/g), the estimated treatment
effects for the UACR <30 mg/g subgroup and UACR =30-300 mg/g subgroup were similar to
the overall treatment effects for both doses, while the estimated treatment effect for the
UACR >300 mg/g subgroup appeared to be larger with wider confidence interval, visually
deviating from the other two subgroups for both doses relative to placebo. Given the small
sample size of the UACR >300 subgroup and that the treatment effect favored aprocitentan
(Table 9), no further analyses were warranted.
For baseline eGFR categories the estimated treatment effect appeared to be smaller than the
overall treatment effect in subjects with eGFR>=60 mL/min /1.73 m2 and greater than the overall
treatment effect in subjects with eGFR 15-60 mL/min /1.73 m2. This finding was particularly
notable for the 25 mg dose of aprocitentan, i.e., the confidence intervals of the treatment effects
for the two CKD subgroups did not overlap and the estimated treatment effect for the subgroup
with eGFR>=60 mL/min /1.73 m2 was much smaller (LSM difference = -1.26) than the overall
treatment effect. Additional subgroup analyses were conducted for baseline eGFR categories
using different blood pressure endpoints (including uAOBPM SiDBP, 24h ABPM SiSBP and
SiDBP) in Part 1 and data from Part 3. The results (see Section 16) suggest that the findings for
the primary efficacy endpoint in eGFR subgroups may be due to chance and not reflect a true
difference.
Shrinkage Estimates
There are typically some random highs and random lows in sample estimates of subgroup
treatment effects due to small sample sizes and large variability for some subgroups. Therefore,
we also derived shrinkage estimates of subgroup treatment effects using a Bayesian hierarchical
38
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
model based on summary sample estimates. This approach leads to improved precision and
lower variability, shown with narrower confidence intervals, most notably in subgroups with a
smaller sample size. The total variability in the sample estimates is the sum of the within
subgroup variability of the sample estimator and the across subgroups variability in the
underlying/true parameter values. A shrinkage estimate of the subgroup treatment effect, which
borrows information from the other subgroups while estimating the treatment effect for a specific
subgroup, is a “weighted” average of the sample estimate and overall estimate. A set of fairly
noninfluential priors [𝜇~Normal (0, 102), 𝜏2~HalfNormal(0, 102) with lower limit of 10-12] were
used to derive shrinkage estimates for all subgroups. These results showed narrower confidence
intervals and more consistent results across different subgroups.

TRYVIO (aprocitentan)

Summary of Findings in Part 1
Part 1 assessed the efficacy of two doses of aprocitentan in reducing SiSBP using uAOBPM
obtained at trough. Both doses were statistically superior to placebo in reducing SiSBP at
Week 4. The size of the placebo-corrected treatment effect, which was similar in the 12.5 mg and
25 mg dose arms (a reduction of 3.8 mmHg and 3.7 mmHg, respectively), is considered
clinically meaningful. Sensitivity analyses confirmed the robustness of the primary analysis
results. For the most part, treatment effects were consistent across prespecified subgroups for
both dose arms for the prespecified primary endpoint. However, for the UACR and eGFR
subgroups, interaction p-values indicated potential heterogeneity. In addition, although the
interaction p-value for the race variable did not suggest heterogeneity among race subgroups, the
subgroup analyses showed neutral effects for Black or African American subjects for both
aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg. Further analyses were conducted to explore these findings.
These additional analyses suggested that the extreme large or small treatment effects observed in
these subgroups were due to small sample sizes and randomness.
Results of Part 3
Part 3 of the study consisted of a 12-week double-blind period in which subjects were
rerandomized to placebo or remained on aprocitentan. Subjects that completed treatment through
Parts 1 and 2 were then rerandomized to aprocitentan 25 mg or placebo in Part 3. Of the 730
subjects randomized in Part 1, there were 614 subjects randomized to Part 3. This is more than
the planed sample size of 380, the Applicant stated that the larger-than-planned sample size was
caused by the addition of sites and countries in 2020. These additional sites and countries were
meant to compensate for the lower recruitment due to the Covid‑19 pandemic..
Demographic
Baseline demographics for Part 3 were generally balanced among the treatment arms (Table 17).
A greater proportion of subjects <65 years of age were randomized to the placebo group;
however, the mean and median age was similar in the two arms. Overall, the demographics of
the subjects who were rerandomized in Part 3 were similar to those of subjects in Part 1 (Table
8).

Part 3 Disposition
Of the 614 rerandomized subjects, 36 (6%) discontinued study drug during Part 3, and 22 (3.6%)
discontinued the study (Table 19). The most frequently reported reasons for treatment
discontinuation were adverse event [15 (2.4%)] and “other” [15 (2.4%)]. There was one death in
the aprocitentan 25 mg group due to multiple organ dysfunction syndrome after the Week 40
analysis visit.

Analyses for the Secondary Endpoint
The secondary endpoint of change in SiSBP from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 was
analyzed using an MMRM analysis. The results of the prespecified secondary analysis and the
FDA recommended analysis (see “Missing Data Handling and FDA Recommended Primary
Analysis” for further details) are shown in Table 20. Statistical significance was demonstrated in
both analyses and both approaches resulted in a similar estimate of the treatment effect [LSM
difference: -5.7 mmHg; 95% CI: -7.8, -3.6; p-value<0.0001 based on the FDA recommended
analysis].

Missing Data Handling and FDA Recommended Secondary Analysis
Similar to the primary endpoint analysis, missing data were handled by MMRM in the
prespecified main analysis of the secondary endpoint and assumed to be missing at random. At
Week 40, 46 (15%) and 40 (13%) subjects were missing SiSBP measurements in the
aprocitentan 25 mg group and placebo group, respectively, resulting in a 14% overall missing
rate for Week 40 (Table 21). There were 22 subjects (4%) missing both Week 38 and Week 40
data. These 22 subjects were not included in the MMRM analysis for the secondary efficacy
endpoint, raising the same concerns as those discussed for the primary analysis. As such, the
FDA conducted an alternative analysis using similar methods as those used in the FDA analysis
of the primary endpoint (i.e., a mixture of standard MI and J2R-MI) to obtain an estimate of the
treatment effect in Part 3 that more appropriately handles missing data (see Table 20).

Sensitivity analyses similar to those performed for the primary endpoint were also performed for
the secondary endpoint (change in SiSBP from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40). One
additional sensitivity analysis was performed because of the high missing rate at Week 40. The
main reason for the high missing rate (14%) at Week 40 was that 62 subjects performed the
Week 40 visits outside of the Week 40 analysis window (Day 21 to Day 41 after
rerandomization). The main analysis used actual analysis visits and considered these Week 40
visits as missing. Thus, a supplementary analysis was performed based on visits as per protocol
(not analysis windows).
The results of these sensitivity analyses are shown in Table 22. As shown in the table, the LSM
differences for all of the sensitivity/supplementary analyses (as well as the main analysis result in
Table 20) were larger than the LSM differences seen in Part 1. Because the actual sample size of
614 was much larger than the planned sample size of 380, the study was overpowered to detect
aprocitentan’s effect on blood pressure during the randomized withdrawal phase of the trial
(assumed in power calculations to be 5 mmHg), which likely contributed to the high statistical
significance of the p-values shown in the table. However, given that the estimated treatment
effect during the randomized withdrawal phase was larger than in Part 1, as well as the results of
the sensitivity/supplementary analyses, the secondary endpoint findings are considered to be
robust and to support the conclusion that aprocitentan’s blood pressure lowering effect is
durable.

Subgroup Analyses
Subgroup analyses, including shrinkage analyses (where the same set of noninfluential priors as
for the subgroup analyses of primary endpoint were used), were also performed for the
secondary endpoint. Overall, the treatment effects were consistent across subgroups for the
secondary endpoint (Figure 6). The Hispanic or Latino subgroup showed a treatment effect in the
unfavorable direction, however, given the small sample size (and thus wide confidence interval)
of this subgroup and the large treatment effect shown in Part 1 for aprocitentan 25 mg versus
placebo, it is likely that this unfavorable effect is not a real finding and reflects a random low
value.

Abbreviations: CKD, chronic kidney disease, eGFR, estimated glomerular filtration rate, F, female; M, male, MMRM, mixed models
for repeated measures UACR, urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio; vs., versus

Summary of Findings in Part 3
The persistence of the BP-lowering effect of aprocitentan was demonstrated in Part 3 of the
study, with increased mean SiSBP in subjects rerandomized to placebo, and maintained mean
SiSBP in subjects rerandomized to aprocitentan 25 mg. The robustness of the main analysis was
supported by the sensitivity analyses. Subgroup findings were, as a whole, consistent with the
findings in the overall population.

2 Page(s) have been Withheld in Full as b4 (CCI/TS) immediately following this page

47
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
aprocitentan is a major active metabolite of macitentan and represents 50% and approximately
90% of the combined exposure to macitentan and aprocitentan in rats and rabbits, respectively.
The key safety findings from nonclinical studies and their potential clinical relevance are
discussed below. The exposure multiples in the discussion that follows were calculated based on
the AUC (34.8 µg.hr/mL) at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 12.5 mg/day.
For a more detailed summary of nonclinical study, refer to Section 13.
Embryo-Fetal Toxicities
In embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) toxicity studies, treatment with macitentan (for which
aprocitentan is a major metabolite) in pregnant rats and rabbits revealed teratogenicity and
developmental toxicity at all doses tested, and therefore a no observed adverse effect level
(NOAEL) for embryo fetal toxicity in the rat and rabbits could not be established. These findings
were consistent with findings with other members of the ERA class. The relevant animal data are
summarized below.
•

In pregnant rats, oral administration of 3, 10, and 150 mg/kg/day macitentan during the
period of major organogenesis resulted in fetal abnormalities, which included cardiovascular
and mandibular arch fusion malformations, at all doses tested. The aprocitentan AUC at the
lowest macitentan dose tested (3 mg/kg/day) was approximately equivalent to the clinal
exposure at the MRHD of 12.5 mg/day, based on AUC.

•

In pregnant rabbits, oral administration of 2.5, 12.5, and 25 mg/kg/day macitentan during the
period of major organogenesis resulted in fetal abnormalities, which included cardiovascular
and mandibular arch fusion malformations, at all doses tested. The aprocitentan exposure at
lowest dose tested (2.5 mg/kg/day) was approximately 15-fold the clinical exposure at the
MRHD, based on AUC.

•

In a pre- and postnatal developmental study in female rats, oral administration of 10, 50 or
250 mg/kg/day macitentan during the pregnancy and the lactational periods reduced pup
survival and impaired male fertility of offspring at all doses. The aprocitentan exposure at the
lowest dose (10 mg/kg/day) was approximately 2-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD,
based on AUC.

Following submission of the aprocitentan NDA, the Applicant submitted additional EFD toxicity
studies in which aprocitentan itself was administered to animals. No embryo-fetal toxicity was
observed in the EFD studies with aprocitentan alone at 5-fold and 29-fold the human exposure at
the MRHD in rats and rabbits, respectively. The highest dose of aprocitentan tested in the rat
EFD study was too low to identify a lowest-observed adverse effect level, a critical attribute of
an EFD study for a drug with a teratogenic mechanism of action. Indeed, the rat study tested
doses only up to 5-fold the clinical exposure, based on AUC. The mechanism of action for ERAs
is well established to be teratogenic in animals. In the absence of direct comparisons to other
ERAs at their relevant clinical exposures, the available information is insufficient to conclude
(b) (4)
that
. In conclusion,
without studies that produced exposures resulting in the expected embryo-fetal toxicities, and
without inclusion of a macitentan (or other ERA) comparator, the available data are insufficient
to characterize the embryofetal risk of aprocitentan. Therefore, the risk assessment based on the
51
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
studies conducted with macitentan continues to represent the most appropriate data for
communicating the reproductive risk associated with aprocitentan. Reproductive risk could be
reevaluated when adequately designed EFD studies with aprocitentan that include macitentan or
other ERA as a comparator are provided by the Applicant.
Fertility
Male Fertility
In a fertility study in male rats given aprocitentan for 15 weeks (including 10-week premating
period, 2-week mating period and 3-week post-mating period) at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day
(approximately 52-fold the exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC), no aprocitentan related
effects on male gonadal function and mating behavior were observed. Likewise, no significant
aprocitentan-related adverse effects on reproductive endpoints, including testicular and
epididymal sperm count, sperm motility or morphology parameters, were observed. Minimal
dilation of the seminiferous tubules, but not tubular degeneration or atrophy, was observed in the
testes at ≥50 mg/kg/day, which represents approximately 13-fold the exposure at the MRHD
based on AUC.
In the repeat-dose toxicology studies in male rats and dogs given aprocitentan for 26 weeks or 39
weeks, respectively, at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day, an increased incidence of tubular
degeneration/atrophy was observed at 250 mg/kg/day in rats and at ≥25 mg/kg/day in dogs,
representing approximately 41-fold and 53-fold, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD, based
on AUC. The tubular findings in the testes were resolved at the end of the recovery period in
both species. Reversible minimal dilation of seminiferous tubules of the testes were observed at
doses ≥50 mg/kg/day in rats and ≥5 mg/kg/day in dogs. These findings were not associated with
degeneration or atrophy and were not considered adverse. The NOAEL was thus established at
50 mg/kg/day in rats and 5 mg/kg/day in dogs, which represent approximately 14- and 11-fold,
respectively, the exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
In animal studies with several other ERAs, including bosentan, ambrisentan and macitentan
(Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2001; Gilead Sciences 2007; Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2013),
testicular tubular atrophy was observed in rodents, similar to what was observed with
aprocitentan. Adverse effects on sperm count and male fertility were reported in rats treated with
ambrisentan at high exposures, but similar findings were not reported with macitentan
(Gilead Sciences 2007; Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2013). Decreased sperm counts were
reported in patients receiving bosentan (Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2001), but clinical sperm
evaluations are not available for ambrisentan and macitentan. Given that the testicular tubular
degeneration/atrophy observed in rats and dogs treated with aprocitentan occurred at high
exposures (>41-fold of aprocitentan exposure at the MRHD) and were not seen at or below the
tested doses that provided 14- or 11-fold aprocitentan exposure at the MRHD, the concern for
potential clinical risk might typically be deemed to be low. However, given the clinical risk
identified with bosentan, a potential for clinical fertility risk with aprocitentan in males cannot be
excluded.

52
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Female Fertility
In a fertility and early embryonic development study in female rats given aprocitentan prior to
mating, no effect on gonadal function, mating behavior, or fertility index was observed at doses
up to 250 mg/kg/day. A higher percentage of acyclic females or females with an increased mean
estrus cycle length was observed at 250 mg/kg/day. Mean number of implantation sites and mean
number of corpora lutea was slightly reduced at ≥50 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 23-fold
the AUC at the MRHD for females, based on AUC. The NOAEL for all measured parameters
was determined to be 10 mg/kg/day, which represents 5-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD,
based on AUC. Given that no impairment of fertility function was observed, these findings are
deemed to have limited clinical relevance.
Other Target Organ Toxicity
General toxicity studies were conducted for durations up to 26 weeks in rats and 39 weeks in
dogs. Target organs of toxicity and the clinical relevance of the findings are discussed below.
Liver
In the 26-week toxicity study rats, a dose-dependent increase in liver weight and increasing
incidence/severity of centrilobular hepatocellular hypertrophy and centrilobular fatty change
(with a dark-colored appearance upon gross examination) were observed, which is considered an
adaptive response caused by increased microsomal cytochrome P450 enzyme induction. At
250 mg/kg/day, approximately 41-fold the AUC at the MRHD, a moderate hepatocellular
vacuolar degeneration/necrosis in the hypertrophic hepatocytes with minimal multinucleated
hepatocytes was observed. In the 39-week toxicity study in dogs, an increased incidence of
hepatocellular hypertrophy was also observed at ≥25 mg/kg/day (approximately 53-fold the
AUC at the MRHD). No degenerative changes were observed in dogs at any dose, including the
50 mg/kg/day high dose. No adverse liver changes were observed in rats and dogs at
50 mg/kg/day (approximately 14-fold the AUC at the MRHD) and 75/50 mg/kg/day
(approximately 82-fold the AUC at the MRHD), respectively. Hepatotoxicity has been reported
with other ERAs and was seen in clinical studies with aprocitentan.
Hematological Toxicity
Treatment-related, reversible decreases in hemoglobin, RBC, and hematocrit (packed cell
volume) were observed in female dogs at 75/50 mg/kg/day without any hemolysis,
myelosuppression, or reticulocytes changes. No hematological changes were observed at
25 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 53-fold the AUC at the MRHD. While the large exposure
margins and reversibility typically indicate minimal human risk, anemia has been reported with
other ERAs and was seen in clinical studies with aprocitentan.
Nasal Congestion
Reversible increases in the incidence/severity of nasal congestion associated minimal to
moderate submucosal edema and Goblet cell proliferation were observed at ≥25 mg/kg/day in
dogs. These doses represent ≥53-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC. Nasal
congestion was also noted in the clinical studies at supra-pharmacological doses
(>25 mg/kg/day). Given the foregoing, the human risk is considered to be low.
53
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Genetic Toxicology
There was no evidence of mutagenicity or clastogenicity for aprocitentan in a standard battery of
tests that included in vitro bacterial reverse mutation and chromosomal aberration assays, and an
in vivo rat micronucleus study.
Carcinogenicity
The carcinogenicity assessment of aprocitentan was based on carcinogenicity studies conducted
with another ERA, macitentan, for which its major metabolite is aprocitentan. No
carcinogenicity studies were conducted with aprocitentan alone. Two-year carcinogenicity
studies with macitentan did not identify any carcinogenic potential at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day
and 250 mg/kg/day in mice and rats, where aprocitentan exposures represented approximately
30-fold and 11-fold, respectively, the clinical aprocitentan exposure at the MRHD based on
AUC.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Aprocitentan is an ERA. Risks described in labeling for approved ERAs include embryo-fetal
toxicity, hepatotoxicity, pulmonary edema with pulmonary veno-occlusive disease, decreased
sperm counts, decreases in hemoglobin and hematocrit, and fluid retention. To date, all ERAs
have been approved with a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) for embryo-fetal
toxicity, and two of the ERAs, bosentan and sparsentan, also include risk mitigation for hepatotoxicity in the REMS.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Aprocitentan is neither approved nor marketed in the United States or worldwide.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The safety review included a review of data quality, as well as adverse events (AEs), laboratory
data, and vital sign datasets. AEs were analyzed by the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory
Activities (MedDRA) [version 24.1] preferred term and by pooling similar AEs (referred to as
the MedDRA SMQ or Food and Drug Administration Medical Dictionary for Regulatory
Activities query [FMQ]). The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity
issues that raised concern. No major issues were identified with respect to recording, coding, and
categorizing AEs, and the translation of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms for the AEs
reported in PRECISION was found to be acceptable.
The FDA safety review focused on the data collected in the PRECISION study. PRECISION
included three treatment periods (Part 1, 2, and 3) with differing designs and durations, thus, the
three treatment periods were analyzed separately. Adverse events were collected up through the
54
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
safety follow-up period, which started on the day after the last dose of study treatment and ended
with a safety follow-up visit 30–33 days later. Adverse events of special interest (AESIs)
included treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) suggestive of anemia, edema/fluid
retention, decompensation of heart failure, and hepatic injury. Since the target population has
multiple cardio-cerebrovascular risk factors, an additional safety topic of interest included
treatment-emergent major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE), defined as cardiovascular
death, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke, and MACE-plus, defined as
hospitalization for heart failure, which were adjudicated by an independent central adjudication
committee (CAC).
Safety data for the initial treatment period (Part 1; 4-week placebo-controlled) are presented in
the following sections. For AESIs and any TEAEs that warranted further evaluation, data from
study Parts 2 and 3 are also presented if pertinent.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
In the PRECISION study safety population, 724 subjects received at least one dose of
aprocitentan, and the mean duration of exposure was 36.6 weeks (Table 24). Total subject
exposures were sufficient to assess the safety of aprocitentan for the proposed indication and
dose.

7.6.2. Deaths
There were 11 treatment-emergent deaths (1.3%) during the study, all reported in aprocitentantreated subjects. Of these deaths, one was reported in Part 1, nine were reported during Part 2 and
one was reported in Part 3 (Table 26). Five of the deaths were attributed to COVID-19 (Part 1,
n=1; Part 2, n=3; Part 3, n=1), one death was attributed to a colon perforation from a
colonoscopy (Part 2), and one death was attributed to invasive breast carcinoma (primary cause
of death) with concurrent subdural hemorrhage and brain edema (Part 2). The remaining four
deaths were adjudicated as MACE, all reported in Part 2 with no comparator arm. Based on a
review of the cases as well as what is known about the pharmacologic class, it seems unlikely
that aprocitentan played a role in these deaths. See Section 7.6.6 for further discussion of MACE.
Table 139 provides the listing of subject deaths; the narratives for the deaths adjudicated as
MACE are provided in Table 140.

7.6.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
In Part 1 of PRECISION, SAEs were uncommon but reported in a slightly higher proportion of
aprocitentan-treated subjects (3% in each dosing arm) as compared to placebo (1%). With the
exception of the Thrombosis FMQ (narrow), the risk difference versus placebo was less than 1%
for specific preferred terms or groupings (Table 27). For the Thrombosis FMQ, although the risk
difference was 1.2% for the 12.5 mg arm, the risk difference was -0.4% for the 25 mg arm.

arm; n, number of subjects with adverse event; SAE, serious adverse event; SOC, system organ class; TIA, transient ischemic attack; vs., versus

7.6.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment
Discontinuation
AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of study treatment were uncommon in Part 1 of the study but reported in a slightly higher
proportion of aprocitentan-treated patients (3% and 2%, respectively, for the 12.5 mg and 25 mg dose) as compared to placebo (1%;
Table 29). In Part 2, AEs leading to treatment discontinuation were reported in 3.8% of subjects; the proportion was slightly higher
among subjects who switched from the placebo arm (in Part 1) (5.1%) than among subjects who switched from the 12.5 mg arm
(2.6%) or remained on the 25 mg dose (3.8%; Table 146). After review of the cases, AEs leading to discontinuation due to edema,
elevated transaminases, and hypersensitivity appear to be related to aprocitentan. See Sections 7.6.5.1, 7.6.5.3, and 7.6.5.4 for further
discussion of these events, respectively. AEs resulting in discontinuation that were adjudicated as MACE are discussed in Section
7.6.6 and narratives are provided in Table 144.

TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
7.6.5.1. Edema/Fluid Retention
Fluid retention is a known risk of ERAs. Preferred terms for edema-related AEs were pooled via
grouped query and included fluid retention, oedema peripheral, face oedema, pulmonary
oedema, and localised oedema. In Part 1 of the PRECISION study, an imbalance in edema
(grouped query) was observed between the aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg arms (9% and 18%,
respectively) and placebo arm (2%) (Table 31). There appeared to be a dose-response
relationship. Time to onset was as early as the first week of treatment but continued throughout
the study (Figure 7). In Part 2, edema AEs were reported in 17% of subjects (Table 31); the
incidence was higher among subjects who switched from the placebo arm (23%) as compared to
those subjects who switched from the 12.5 mg arm (16%) and those who remained on the 25 mg
dose (12%; Table 143). SAEs of pulmonary edema were reported in one subject in the
aprocitentan 25 mg arm in Part 1 and one subject in Part 2; narratives are provided below. Both
SAEs of pulmonary edema were reported in the context of significantly elevated blood pressure
that most likely precipitated the events.
Overall, the majority of edema events were reported as mild to moderate in severity, with six
subjects discontinuing aprocitentan due to edema (Table 31). For information on changes in body
weight, see Section 7.6.9.1. Subgroup analyses for Part 1 suggested a greater risk in subjects
greater than 65 years old, in subjects taking amlodipine 10 mg as background therapy, and in
subjects with lower levels of kidney function (Table 142).

(b) (6)

Subject
was a 67-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who had a
SAE of pulmonary edema, cardiac failure, and increased blood pressure on Study Day 8-12.
The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of heart failure with preserved
ejection fraction with flash pulmonary edema after experiencing increasing dyspnea. Blood
pressure upon arrival was 220/104 mmHg and chest X-ray showed early pulmonary edema.
The subject’s NT-proBNP value during hospitalization was not reported. The subject
recovered after treatment (blood pressure upon hospital discharge was 123/65 mm/Hg) and
the event was considered not related to the study drug. There was no action taken with study
treatment. On Study Day 23, the subject presented to the emergency department with severe
chest pain and peripheral edema that resolved after treatment with transdermal nitroglycerin
and furosemide. There was no action taken with study treatment and the event was
considered not related to the study drug. Prior to the study (at screening), the subject’s
individual background antihypertensive therapy included valsartan 300 mg and nifedipine 60
mg which were switched to SBAT per study protocol. Blood pressure at baseline was 150/82
mmHg.

(b) (6)

Subject
was a 72-year-old Black male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who
experienced an SAE of pulmonary edema, congestive heart failure, and hypertensive urgency
on Study Day 134 to 137. The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of
congestive heart failure, pulmonary edema in the setting of hypertensive urgency, acute
kidney injury on chronic kidney disease, and atrial flutter. Blood pressure upon arrival was
reported as 209/60 mmHg and chest X-ray showed enlarged cardiac silhouette and
pulmonary vasculature congestion. The subject’s NT-proBNP value during hospitalization
was not reported. The subject recovered after treatment with intravenous furosemide, nitrates,
and hydralazine. Study treatment was interrupted due to the hypertension. The event was
considered not related to study drug. Prior to the study (at screening), the subject’s individual
background antihypertensive therapy included SBAT and hydralazine (50 mg twice daily)
which was discontinued at that time, per study protocol. The subject was restarted on study
treatment on Study Day 147 and completed study Parts 2 and 3 in the aprocitentan 25 mg
arm as scheduled.

Adjudicated MACE-Plus/Heart Failure Hospitalization
MACE-plus was defined as hospitalization for heart failure and adjudicated by the CAC. Over
the course of the PRECISION study, eleven subjects experienced heart failure hospitalization
adjudicated as MACE-plus, two of which were reported in Part 1 in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm
(b) (6)
(Table 32). One of the cases in Part 1 (SAE of pulmonary edema in Subject
) is
discussed in the prior section; the other event that was adjudicated as a heart failure
hospitalization in Part 1 occurred in a subject who had chronic heart failure at baseline, which
was reported as worsening. In Part 2, six subjects experienced events that were adjudicated as a
heart failure hospitalization. Of these subjects, one had previously taken aprocitentan 25 mg
during Part 1 and five had previously been assigned to placebo. Of those who were switched
from placebo, three of the events occurred within the first 3 weeks of initiating aprocitentan. In
Part 3, three subjects experienced events that were adjudicated as heart failure hospitalizations; 2
subjects in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm and 1 subject in the placebo arm (Table 32). Narratives
for the adjudicated MACE-plus heart failure hospitalizations are provided in Table 144. Heart
failure events resulting in discontinuation were uncommon (Table 32).

7.6.5.2. Anemia
Anemia is a known risk of ERAs and is thought to be due in part to hemodilution. In Part 1 of PRECISION, AEs suggestive of anemia
(FMQ narrow) were reported in the aprocitentan arms but not in the placebo arm; however, there was no evidence of a dose-response
relationship (4% and 1% in the 12.5 mg and 25 mg dose groups, respectively; Table 33). In Part 2, anemia AEs were reported in 9% of
aprocitentan-treated. In Part 3, the incidence was similar among treatment groups. All of the AEs were reported to be mild to moderate
(b) (6)
in severity, with the exception of two AEs. In Part 1, one subject (Subject
) experienced severe anemia related to blood loss,
(b) (6)
and in Part 2, one subject (Subject
) experienced severe and serious anemia, which was not associated with a bleeding event
(b) (6)
and resulted in a blood transfusion. Discontinuation of study treatment due to anemia was reported in one subject (Subject
) in

Anemia Narratives
•

Serious AE
(b) (6)

– Subject
was a 72-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who
experienced an SAE of anemia on Study Day 78 to 82. The subject presented to the
emergency department with chest pain which was diagnosed as unstable angina pectoris,
and a coronary artery bypass graft procedure was scheduled. On the same day, the subject
was diagnosed with anemia (reported lab value: Hgb 8.6 g/dL). Fecal occult blood test
was positive; however, no active bleeds were noted on endoscopy and colonoscopy. The
event resolved after treatment with two units of red blood cells. No action was taken with
study treatment and the event was considered not related to study treatment. Hemoglobin
at baseline was 11.0 g/dL and reported as 9.1 g/dL and 9.2 g/dL, at the end of Parts 1 and
2, respectively. The last reported measurement was 9.9 g/dL, which was 2 months after
the last dose of study drug.
•

Resulted in Discontinuation
(b) (6)

– Subject
was a 72-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who
received a blood transfusion for anemia of unknown etiology reported as a nonserious AE
on Study Day 86-89. During screening on Day –69, it was reported that the subject
experienced a nonserious event of anemia of unknown etiology of moderate intensity
(reported value: Hgb 11.6 g/dL). During the study, the subject was treated with
intramuscular cyanocobalamin from Study Day 42-57 (reported lab values: Hgb 8.8 g/dL,
Day 42; Hgb 9.0 g/dL, Day 50; 8.7 g/dL, Day 67) and transfused with one unit of red
blood cells on Study Day 86 (Hgb value not provided). Given the need for blood
transfusion, the criterion for permanent study drug discontinuation was met and
aprocitentan was discontinued on Day 89 (lab values not reported). Four days later on
Day 93, hemoglobin was reported as 9.5 g/dL. The last reported hemoglobin was 12.1
g/dL on Day 121 at the safety follow-up visit.
Hemoglobin Decrease
Compared to the placebo group, the aprocitentan arms had a larger mean decrease from baseline
in hemoglobin at Week 4 of treatment (mean decrease of 0.80 and 0.85 g/dL in aprocitentan
12.5 mg and 25 mg arms, respectively, compared to a decrease of 0.04 g/dL in the placebo arm)
(Figure 8). In subjects reassigned from placebo to the aprocitentan 25 mg arm in Part 2, mean
decreases from Week 4 baseline values were similar to values observed in subjects initially
randomized to 12.5 mg and 25 mg at the start of the study; the mean decreases appeared to
stabilize by Week 6 (Figure 8). In Part 3, mean hemoglobin levels rose in subjects rerandomized
from aprocitentan to placebo, indicating that the effect on hemoglobin was reversible (Figure 9).
