Hemoglobin decreases exceeding specified levels are shown in Table 145. In Part 1, the
incidence of a hemoglobin decrease >2 g/dL compared to baseline was greater in subjects
receiving aprocitentan (7% of 12.5 mg and 3% of 25 mg arms) compared to subjects treated with
placebo (1%). A decrease in hemoglobin from baseline to below 10.0 g/dL in the initial 4-weeks
was reported in 3% of subjects taking aprocitentan 12.5 mg, 2% of subjects taking aprocitentan
25 mg, versus 0 subjects taking placebo (Table 145).
7.6.5.3. Hepatotoxicity
Adverse Events
ERAs can cause hepatoxicity (degree of risk varies among members of the class) and, as such,
hepatotoxicity was an AESI for aprocitentan. During Part 1, no AEs suggestive of hepatic injury
were reported in the aprocitentan arms (Table 34). During Part 2, 6 (0.9%) subjects reported AEs
within the Hepatic injury and Hepatic failure narrow FMQ; of these, Hepatitis B was reported for
one subject and Hepatitis C was reported for one subject. All of these events were reported to be
mild to moderate in severity. In Part 3, AEs with this narrow FMQ were reported in 2.3% of
subjects who were rerandomized to placebo arm as compared to <1% of subjects randomized to
remain in the aprocitentan arm.

passage of gallstones could compete. Her sex and obesity increase her risk of gallstones.
However, she was asymptomatic, and ultrasound did not report gallstones. The Applicant

(b) (6)

Subject
: The subject was a 36-year-old white female in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm
who experienced elevated liver enzymes on Study Day 139; ALT, AST, AP, and TB were
145 U/L (4.3 x ULN), 75 U/L, 54 U/L and 0.29 mg/dL, respectively. CK was normal. No
symptoms were mentioned. The study drug continued without change. On Study Day 147,
ALT and AST peaked at 220 U/L (6.5 x ULN) and 80 U/L, respectively. Still no symptoms
were mentioned. The study drug was held on Study Day 148. The liver enzymes fell to
normal or nearly normal by Day 196. On the same day, aprocitentan 25 mg was restarted. On
Study Day 240 (44 days after rechallenging), ALT and AST were 78 and 37 U/L,
respectively. AP and TB remained < ULN throughout both aminotransferase elevations.
Aprocitentan was permanently discontinued on Day 247 due to the aminotransferase
elevations. ALT and AST rose to second peak of 114 (3.3 x ULN) and 49 U/L on Study Day
253. Thereafter, ALT improved with >50% decline from second peak occurring within 27
days, and AST was back to normal (Figure 11). At baseline, the subject’s BMI was 28 kg/m2.
Relevant medical history included just the targeted disease. Alcohol history was not
provided. Concurrent medications relative to DILI risk were nil. The subject’s ALT, AST,
AP, and TB were 24 U/L, 17 U/L, 50 U/L and 0.2 mg/dL, respectively, at baseline. ALT
return to baseline had not yet occurred at last follow-up. Evaluation testing was not done.

was assessed as related to aprocitentan.

7.6.6. MACE
MACE was defined as cardiovascular death, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or non‑fatal stroke,
and events were adjudicated by the CAC (Table 37). MACE events would be expected in this
population and given the design of the study, data are challenging to interpret. On face, they do
not raise concern. A discussion of the adjudicated MACE deaths is provided in Section 7.6.2.
Narratives for subjects with adjudicated MACE are provided in Table 144.

7.6.7. Pregnancies During the PRECISION Study
(b) (6)

One subject (
) discontinued study treatment due to pregnancy during Part 2 of the
PRECISION study. Estimated exposure to aprocitentan 25 mg was 7 weeks of the first trimester.
A normal baby was born at 38 weeks’ gestation. The narrative is provided below:
•

(b) (6)

Subject
: A 34-year-old White female informed the study site that she tested positive
(b) (6)
on a urine pregnancy test on Study Day 139 (
). The study medication
(aprocitentan 25 mg) and background therapy were permanently discontinued on the same
day. The pregnancy was confirmed by the site (B-hCG was 1157 mIU/mL on Study Day 141).
Her previous B-hCG test was negative on Study Day 90 (49 days prior). The Investigator
explained to her the potential complications which might affect the fetus/baby. She decided
to continue with the pregnancy and was referred to a gynecologist. Three weeks later, the
subject visited the ED for increased BP, reported as 180/90 mmHg, and was started on
labetalol 100mg twice daily. All obstetrical ultrasounds of the fetus/fetal anatomy were
normal, blood tests, genetic tests (Myriad panel and Alpha-Fetoprotein Test) were normal;
(b) (6)
the estimated delivery date was
. She completed her Safety Follow-Up visit
on Study Day 189 with a normal visit and labs reported. At 38-weeks’ gestation, the subject
was admitted for labor induction and endorsed good fetal movement. Cesarean section was
performed due to failure to progress in labor and a male infant was born with weight 8 lbs
13.1 oz. There were no delivery complications, no neonatal illness, no need for resuscitation,
no need for Intensive Care Unit admission, and no congenital anomalies were diagnosed. The
subject was discharged from the hospital with routine postpartum follow-up. Baseline
characteristics for the subject included obesity (BMI 35.2 kg/m2) and a normal eGFR (119
mL/min/1.73m2). Obstetrical history included five previous pregnancies complicated by preeclampsia; one spontaneous abortion followed by four live births delivered at 39 weeks and all
healthy babies. Concomitant medications included ferrous fumarate, escitalopram 10 mg, and
use of three methods of contraception (nonoxynol gel as needed, ethinyl
estradiol/norethisterone acetate tablets daily, and condom usage as needed).

7.6.8. Laboratory Findings
Laboratory findings associated with AESIs are described in Section 7.6.5. Analyses of other
biochemistry data did not reveal findings of concern. A summary of the findings for kidney
function is provided below. Overall, a small decrease in eGFR from baseline was observed in
subjects taking aprocitentan, with the greatest decrease occurring during the initial 6 weeks of
treatment in subjects randomized to aprocitentan 25 mg.
7.6.8.1. Kidney Function
Kidney function analyte values exceeding specified levels for Part 1 are provided in Table 39.
The risk difference versus placebo for a ≥25% decrease in eGFR was higher in the patients
treated with aprocitentan 25 mg (3.1%) compared to those treated with 12.5 mg (0.4%). One
(b) (6)
subject (Subject
) in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm experienced an eGFR decrease of
≥50% compared with none in the other treatment arms. However, the subject was no longer

Other than weight gain, analyses of vital signs data did not reveal any safety concerns. Body
weight changes in Part 1 and information on QT interval (QT) prolongation are provided below.

7.6.9.2. Assessment for Potential QT Prolongation
The potential risk for QT prolongation and Torsade de Pointe with aprocitentan was evaluated in
a thorough QT study (ID-080-108) which was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team
for Cardiac Safety Studies. The thorough QT study was a prospective, single-center, randomized,
double-blind, placebo- and moxifloxacin- controlled, 4-way crossover Phase 1 study to assess the
effect of multiple therapeutic and supratherapeutic doses of aprocitentan on the corrected QT
interval duration in healthy subjects. The supratherapeutic dose (100 mg), which is 8 times the
therapeutic dose (12.5 mg), provided approximately 3-fold high clinical exposure. Data were
analyzed using exposure-response analysis as the primary analysis. The results of the study show
that at 8 times the maximum recommended dose, clinically significant corrected QT interval
prolongation was not observed. Further details can be found in the Interdisciplinary Review
Team Review dated August 14, 2023.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Hepatotoxicity
Issue
The PRECISION study data suggests aprocitentan can cause elevations of transaminases without
hyperbilirubinemia.
Background
Hepatoxicity, including liver failure, has been reported with ERAs and is described in the FDAapproved labeling for these products. Macitentan, the parent compound of aprocitentan, includes
hepatotoxicity as a warning and precaution in the labeling and recommends a baseline
measurement of liver analytes (including total bilirubin) and repeat measurements as clinically
indicated. Two products in the ERA class, bosentan and sparsentan, have a REMS for
hepatotoxicity.
Assessment
There was no imbalance in findings for liver analytes between aprocitentan and placebo
exposure in the PRECISION study or Study 201, although, the randomized periods for
comparison were relatively short at four to eight weeks and latencies for other ERA
hepatotoxicity have been several months. There were no cases of jaundice in PRECISION and
hence no Hy’s Law or cholestatic jaundice cases across all study periods. The characterization of
the risk is limited due to the size of the study population.
Case level analysis of the PRECISION study safety data by DHN suggests that aprocitentan can
cause hepatotoxicity. In Part 2, there were two subjects with liver enzyme elevations of greater
than 5-fold the upper limit of normal which were assessed as highly likely related to the use of
aprocitentan. Both subjects had positive rechallenges. One subject had potential hepatocellular
injury (i.e., predominantly aminotransferase elevation) while the other had a mixed injury (i.e.,
89
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
both aminotransferase and alkaline phosphatase elevation). The enzyme elevations were modest
without hyperbilirubinemia and were resolved or nearly resolved at the last follow-up visit after
aprocitentan discontinuation.
Conclusion
Although data indicate potential risk, the findings in the development program as well as the
findings with macitentan suggest that risk can be adequately mitigated with labeling, which will
include a warning and precaution for hepatotoxicity. Labeling will include a recommendation for
monitoring of liver analytes, to be checked prior to initiation and periodically during treatment.
Aprocitentan will not be recommended for use in patients with decompensated cirrhosis or acute
liver disease. Postmarketing surveillance will include enhanced pharmacovigilance for a period
of 5 years with required submission of all serious cases of liver injury as 15-day alert reports and
summary analyses provided in the periodic safety reports.

7.7.2. Embryo-Fetal Toxicity
Issue
Nonclinical data with macitentan suggest an adverse effect of aprocitentan on the developing
fetus when administered to a pregnant woman. In EFD studies with aprocitentan, while no
embryo-fetal toxicity, including teratogenic effects, were observed at 5-fold and 29-fold the
human exposure at MRHD in rats and rabbits, respectively, the highest dose of aprocitentan
tested in the rat EFD study was too low to fully characterize reproductive risk. There are
uncertainties about the embryo-fetal toxicity risk of ERAs in humans. Data regarding pregnancy
outcomes in first trimester exposure of ERAs are limited.
Background
Female adults of reproductive potential (FRP; ages 18-49 years) are included in the target
population for aprocitentan and represented 5% of the PRECISION study population. The
estimated background risk of major birth defects and miscarriage for the indicated population is
unknown. In the general U.S. population, the estimated background risk of major birth defects is
2-4% and risk of miscarriage is 15-20%. Hypertension during pregnancy is considered to be
high-risk. Prescribers of antihypertensives commonly use inhibitors of the renin-angiotensin
system, particularly angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors and ARBs, in the treatment of
hypertension in FRP. These products have a boxed warning for fetal toxicity when used in the
second and third trimester and should be discontinued as soon as possible when pregnancy is
detected. Valsartan (ARB) was used as background therapy in the PRECISION study.
Assessment
The PRECISION study excluded pregnant subjects and required monthly pregnancy tests and
contraception use. There was one unplanned pregnancy reported during Part 2 of PRECISION
(Study Day 139). The subject had taken aprocitentan 25 mg daily for approximately 6 weeks
(first trimester exposure) prior to discontinuation due to the pregnancy. The outcome was
reported as a normal baby born at 38 weeks gestation (see Section 7.6.6 for details).

90
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Aprocitentan is not proposed as first-line therapy which may limit its use and minimize the risk
in FRP. In addition, all ERA products are approved with a REMS with elements to assure safe
use (ETASU) to mitigate the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity. The Division of Cardiology and
Nephrology and the Division of Risk Management agree that a REMS with ETASU is also
necessary for aprocitentan. However, the REMS for aprocitentan will differ from the REMS for
other ERA products (approved for PAH or IgA nephropathy). The elements agreed upon by
these FDA Divisions for the REMS for aprocitentan consist of ETASU A and B, an
implementation system, and a timetable for submission of assessments. Prescriber certification
and specialized training (ETASU A) on the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity helps ensure all
prescribers are educated on the risk. Specialized training is expected to improve identification of
at-risk patients and encourage healthcare providers to counsel patients about the risk and the
actions to take to prevent fetal exposure and minimize exposure during pregnancy. Pharmacy
certification (ETASU B) helps ensure only certified pharmacies are dispensing aprocitentan after
verifying that prescribers of aprocitentan are certified. The REMS for aprocitentan is structured
to ease healthcare burden while supporting the safe use of aprocitentan. For a detailed
description, see the REMS review by Cristen Lambert, PharmD.
In addition to the REMS, the risk will be included in labeling as a Boxed Warning and approval
will include a postmarketing requirement for a descriptive study of pregnancy outcomes.
Conclusion
The risk of embryo-fetal toxicity with aprocitentan can be mitigated with the use of effective
contraception in FRP. A REMS with ETASU is required to ensure the benefits outweigh the risk;
the ETASU is necessary to ensure prescribers are aware of the risk and the need to counsel
patients on the use of contraception and regular pregnancy testing to prevent and minimize fetal
exposure to aprocitentan. Pharmacy certification is necessary to confirm that the prescriber is
certified in the REMS prior to dispensing of aprocitentan to patients. Further, the risk will be
communicated in labeling and an observational pregnancy safety study will be required to assist
in further characterizing the risk.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Renal Impairment
A 50 mg dose of aprocitentan was studied in subjects with severe renal impairment and
compared to age-, sex-, body weight-, and height-matched subjects with normal renal function in
Study AC-080-105. The maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) was similar between groups,
with a geometric mean ratio of 1.04 (90% CI 0.85-1.28). The time to maximum concentration
(Tmax) increased from 5 hours in healthy controls to 7.6 hours in subjects with severe renal
impairment. A lower clearance led to a longer half-life in those with severe renal impairment,
increasing from 47.4 hours in healthy subjects to 53.2 hours in those with severe renal
impairment. The AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ were 32% and 34% higher, respectively, in those with
severe renal impairment compared to healthy subjects. Please see Section 14.2 for a full
91
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
discussion of results. No dose adjustment is required in patients with mild to severe renal
impairment. Because the effect of kidney failure or dialysis on aprocitentan pharmacokinetics
has not been studied, use of aprocitentan in these patients is not recommended.
Hepatic Impairment
A 25 mg dose of aprocitentan was studied in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment and
compared to age-, sex-, body weight-, and height-matched subjects with normal hepatic function
in Study ID-080-109. The Cmax was similar between groups, with a geometric mean ratio of 1.03
(90% CI 0.86-1.24). The Tmax was 4 hours for healthy controls and those with moderate hepatic
impairment. The mean half-life increased from 48.3 hours in healthy subjects to 56.4 hours in
those with moderate hepatic impairment. The AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ were 22% and 23% higher,
respectively, in those with moderate hepatic impairment compared to healthy subjects. Please see
Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. Based on the results, no dose adjustment is required
in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment. Because the effect of severe hepatic
impairment on aprocitentan pharmacokinetics has not been studied, use of aprocitentan in these
patients is not recommended.
Age
Elderly subjects (inclusion criteria: 65-80 years, inclusive) generally had higher Cmax and AUCτ
values in Study AC-080-101. After multiple doses, the Cmax and AUCτ ratio of geometric means
were 1.31 and 1.21 for elderly compared to nonelderly subjects (inclusion criteria: 18 to 45
years, inclusive), respectively. Please see Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. No dose
adjustment is recommended for elderly subjects taking aprocitentan.
Race
A 25 mg dose of aprocitentan was studied in healthy Japanese and Caucasian subjects in Study
ID-080-107. Overall, after multiple doses, the Cmax and AUCτ ratio of geometric means were
1.02 and 1.08 for Japanese subjects compared to Caucasian subjects, respectively. Please see
Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. No dose adjustment is recommended based on race
for aprocitentan.
Sex
Females generally had higher plasma concentrations compared to males in Study AC-080-101.
After a single dose, the Cmax and AUC0-∞ ratio of geometric means were 1.25 and 1.27 for
females compared to males, respectively. After multiple doses, the Cmax and AUCτ ratio of
geometric means were 1.38 and 1.37, respectively, for females compared to males. Please see
Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. No dose adjustment is recommended for females
taking aprocitentan.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
Drug Interactions
Metabolic Pathway
Based on the mass balance study (Study AC-080-104), two metabolic pathways contribute to
>25% of aprocitentan elimination – glucosidation of aprocitentan to metabolite M3 by UGT1A1
and UGT2B7 (accounts for approximately 25% of the administered dose), and hydrolysis of
aprocitentan to metabolite M1 which then undergoes several other reactions leading to
metabolites A1, A4, A5, M4, M5, M6, M11, and M13 (accounts for approximately 31.5% of the
administered dose).
While M1 accounts for 31.5% of the administered dose, based on results from in vitro studies, it
is believed that M1 is formed by both chemical and enzymatic hydrolysis. Incubation of
radiolabeled aprocitentan with human liver microsomes led formation of M1 (4.3% of total
chromatogram radioactivity); however, M1 was also observed in experiments without
microsomes or a nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate-regenerating system (present at
9.3% and 6.4% total chromatogram radioactivity, respectively, in comparable conditions). When
incubated with hepatocytes, M1-M6 was generated, but no metabolite contributed to more than
5% of total chromatogram radioactivity. M1 was also seen in the control experiment without
liver cells and accounted for 8.7% of total chromatogram radioactivity.
For a complete discussion of aprocitentan metabolism, please see in vitro studies in Section 14.1
and the mass-balance study in Section 14.2.
Effect of Other Drugs on Aprocitentan
As glucosidation of aprocitentan to metabolite M3 accounts for approximately 25.2% of the
administered dose, uridine diphosphate-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) inducers may lead to a
decrease in aprocitentan concentration and efficacy; however, no clinical drug-drug interaction
studies were conducted with aprocitentan and UGT inducers.
Effect of Aprocitentan on Other Drugs
The effect of 25 mg aprocitentan at steady state was evaluated on a single dose of 10 mg
rosuvastatin, a breast cancer resistance protein substrate, in healthy male subjects. Aprocitentan
increased the Cmax of rosuvastatin by 40%. The AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ were minimally affected
(geometric mean ratios of 1.07 and 0.99, respectively). The mean half-life of rosuvastatin
decreased from 17.9 hours to 14.1 hours, though this change was not statistically significant
(p=0.14).
The effect of a 50 mg aprocitentan dose at steady state concentrations was evaluated on a single
dose of 8 mg midazolam, a cytochrome P450 3A4 substrate, in healthy male subjects.
Aprocitentan did not have an effect on the Cmax of midazolam (ratio of geometric means 1.04,
90% CI 0.88-1.23); however, the Cmax of 1-hydroxy midazolam was decreased (ratio of
geometric means 0.86, 90% CI 0.7-1.1). The mean half-life for midazolam was unchanged when
administered with aprocitentan; however, the mean half-life of 1-hydroxy midazolam increased
93
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
to a mean of 7.7 hours when administered with aprocitentan compared to 5.8 hours when
midazolam was administered alone. The AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ of midazolam were minimally
increased when administered with aprocitentan (both ratios of geometric means were 1.14). The
AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ of 1-hydroxy midazolam were minimally decreased when administered with
aprocitentan (geometric mean ratios of 0.95 and 0.98, respectively).
Dosing Recommendations
No dose adjustments related to drug interactions are proposed. UGT inducers may decrease
aprocitentan concentrations and thus may lower the efficacy of aprocitentan, though the extent to
which aprocitentan concentration and efficacy may decrease has not been studied. UGT inducers
may not produce any clinically meaningful effect on aprocitentan concentration, as only 25.2%
of the dose is metabolized though glucosidation, and multiple UGT enzymes are involved in
aprocitentan glucosidation. If aprocitentan is an ineffective treatment in patients on UGT
inducers, these patients will likely be treated as other nonresponders, and discontinuation is
likely. Therefore, labeling language for those on UGT inducers is not necessary.
Food Effect
In Study AC-080-101, effects of food on plasma PK variables were assessed after a single
100 mg dose of aprocitentan. Exposure increased with food, with the geometric mean ratios
(fed/fasted) of 1.73 and 1.23 for Cmax and AUCτ-∞, respectively. The Tmax decreased from 9 to 5
hours when fed, though the range was similar (4 to 10 hours for fasted, and 5 to 12 hours for
fed). The labeling states that aprocitentan can be taken with or without food. The labeling is in
line with the Phase 3 study where subjects were to take aprocitentan each morning, irrespective
of food intake.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
The investigational product triggers the Pediatric Research Equity Act as a new active ingredient
for the proposed indication. The Applicant submitted their agreed upon Agreed Initial Pediatric
Study Plan requesting a full waiver of pediatric assessments because “a favorable therapeutic
benefit/risk profile is not expected,” other “safe and efficacious therapeutic classes of
antihypertensive drugs can be used,” aprocitentan does not represent a meaningful therapeutic
benefit over existing therapies, and because of the potential for teratogenicity.
Based on the available data, the Division and Pediatric Review Committee agree that a full
waiver of pediatric studies should be granted because the product fails to represent a meaningful
therapeutic benefit over existing therapies for pediatric patients and is unlikely to be used in a
substantial number of pediatric patients with hypertension. Given the product’s safety profile,
aprocitentan will not be approved as initial (first-line) therapy for the treatment of hypertension
in adults. While the development program demonstrated that the product is effective in lowering
blood pressure in adults, the size of the effect was modest (LS mean difference between
aprocitentan and placebo of ~4 mmHg). Given these issues, the benefit-risk profile is expected to
be unfavorable for pediatric patients with hypertension.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
Embryo-fetal toxicities in rats and rabbits, the results of male and female fertility studies in rats,
and potential reproductive organ toxicities are discussed in Section 7.1. Nonclinical data
supporting labeling language are shown in the table below by labeling section.
Table 40. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy and Lactation
Labeling Section
Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy
Animal data
In pregnant rats, oral administration of macitentan at doses of 3, 10 and
150 mg/kg/day throughout organogenesis, resulted in fetal abnormalities, which
included defects in the cardiovascular and mandibular arch fusion malformations,
at all the doses tested. The aprocitentan exposure at the lowest dose tested
(3 mg/kg/day) was approximately equivalent to the clinical exposure at the MRHD
based on AUC.
In pregnant rabbits, oral administration of macitentan at doses of 2.5, 12.5 and
25 mg/kg/day throughout organogenesis, resulted in fetal abnormalities, which
included defects in the cardiovascular and mandibular arch fusion malformation,
at all the doses tested. The aprocitentan exposure at the lowest dose tested
(2.5 mg/kg/day) was approximately 15-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD
based on AUC.

8.2 Lactation

8.3 Females and
Males of
Reproductive
Potential
13.1 Impairment of
Fertility

In the pre- and postnatal developmental (PPND) study in rats, oral administration
of macitentan at doses of 10, 50 or 250 mg/kg/day during pregnancy and the
lactational period resulted in reduced pup survival and impairment of the male
fertility of the offspring at all doses. The aprocitentan exposure at the lowest dose
tested (10 mg/kg/day) was approximately 2-fold the clinical exposure at the
MRHD based on AUC.
In rats, aprocitentan, which is an active metabolite of macitentan, was
consistently observed in milk samples across all time points after oral
administration of 14C-labeled macitentan.
Other ERAs have shown an adverse effect on spermatogenesis in humans
and/or animals. Aprocitentan may impair fertility in males of reproductive
potential. It is not known whether the effects on fertility would be reversible.
In a fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in female rats, oral
administration of aprocitentan prior to mating at doses of 10, 50, or
250 mg/kg/day resulted in minimally-increased pre-implantation loss at
≥50 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 23-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD
based on AUC at 250 mg/kg/day. No impact on fertility was observed at
10 mg/kg/day, which represents 5-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based
on AUC.
In male rats, oral administration of aprocitentan for 15 weeks had no effect on
fertility or spermatogenesis at doses up to 250 mg/kg, which is approximately 52fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC. In repeat dose toxicity
studies, oral administration of aprocitentan resulted in testicular tubular
degeneration/atrophy in male rats and dogs at the high dose of 250 mg/kg/day
and at doses ≥25 mg/kg/day, respectively, which is approximately 41- and 53-fold
the AUC at the MRHD, respectively. The testicular toxicity was not evident in
male rats and dogs at 50 mg/kg/day and 5 mg/kg/day, respectively, which

9. Product Quality
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed NDA 217686 with respect to
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls and has determined that it meets all applicable standards
to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such, the Office of
Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.

9.1. Device or Combination Product
Considerations
Not applicable.

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site
and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
The Applicant has adequately disclosed financial arrangements with clinical investigators and
the PRECISION study appears to have been conducted in compliance with U.S. regulations
pertaining to Good Clinical Practice. No clinical sites were inspected. The primary efficacy
findings were assessed after a randomized, double-blind treatment period and were based on
objective and standardized measurements. None of the PRECISION study sites enrolled a large
number of study subjects or drove the primary efficacy findings and review of the data did not
raise other serious concerns that would warrant a clinical site inspection (e.g., suspicion of fraud,
scientific misconduct, significant human subject protection violations or adverse event profiles).

11. Advisory Committee Summary
The application provides substantial evidence of the product’s effectiveness in lowering blood
pressure. Given the safety profile of aprocitentan, the product will not be approved as initial
(first-line) therapy for the treatment of hypertension in adults. Given the intended use as well as
the measures that could be put in place to mitigate risk, the application was not felt to raise
significant or controversial issues that would merit outside expertise or public discussion. As
such, no Advisory Committee Meeting was held for this application.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)

III. Additional Analyses and Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
ACT-132577, later called aprocitentan, was developed by Actelion Pharmaceuticals, Ltd.
(Actelion) for the treatment of resistant hypertension under IND 122772. The IND was opened
on October 17, 2014, with a single-center, three-part, phase 1 study to investigate the safety,
tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of ACT-132577 in healthy male and
female subjects. The study was deemed safe to proceed on November 20, 2014.
On June 19, 2017, sponsorship of the IND was transferred from Actelion to Idorsia
Pharmaceuticals Ltd. (Idorsia) who continued developing aprocitentan for the treatment of
hypertension, to lower blood pressure, in adult subjects who are not adequately controlled on
three antihypertensive drugs from different classes, including a diuretic. Idorsia did not develop
aprocitentan as a first-line monotherapy but rather as a background of other antihypertensives.
Aprocitentan’s clinical development program mainly consisted of 10 clinical pharmacology
studies and 2 studies in subjects with hypertension:
•

Study AC-080A201: A phase 2, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-finding study
investigating the blood pressure reduction effect of aprocitentan as monotherapy in naïve or
antihypertensive subjects

•

Study 080A301: A multi-center, blinded, randomized, parallel-group, phase 3 study with
aprocitentan in subjects with resistant hypertension (PRECISION)

The Division of Cardiology and Nephrology (DCN) provided feedback on several aspects of
product development via advice letters dated August 17, 2015, January 12, 2018, November 23,
2018, April 29, 2019, July 17, 2019, November 13, 2019, June 15, 2020, November 4, 2020, and
December 02, 2021. Additional information on milestone meetings and key events may be found
in the below table.

On December 19, 2022, pursuant to Section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic
Act, Idorsia submitted a marketing application for aprocitentan relying on the results of
PRECISION. The review classification was Standard, and the user fee goal date was December
19, 2023.
Along with the original NDA submission, Idorsia submitted the proposed proprietary name
TRYVIO. This name was considered conditionally acceptable by the Division of Medication
Error Prevention and Analysis (DMEPA). A Grant Letter was issued on March 16, 2023.
After consultation with the REMS Oversight Committee (ROC), the Division of Risk
(b) (4)
Management (DRM) and DCN concluded that the
REMS proposed by
Idorsia was not adequate to mitigate the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity associated with
aprocitentan. It was determined that a REMS with Elements to Assure Safe Use (ETASU) was
necessary to mitigate this risk. On August 8, 2023, the Agency shared this decision with Idorsia
asking them to submit a revised REMS to the NDA file. On September 6, 2023, Idorsia
submitted the revised REMS. The September 6, 2023, submission constituted a major
amendment to the application. The user fee goal date was extended by three months; making the
new goal date March 19, 2024.
Of note, in the agreed iPSP dated March 16, 2018, Idorsia requested a full waiver of the
requirement to submit pediatric assessments for aprocitentan because a favorable therapeutic
benefit/risk profile is not expected in the pediatric population. Specifically, given that
aprocitentan was developed as a 4th-line add-on therapy in patients with difficult-to-control
hypertension and due to the safety concerns of Endothelin Receptor Antagonists (ERAs), the use
of aprocitentan was not justified in any age group of the pediatric population with hypertension.
The application was discussed with the Pediatric Review Committee (PeRC) on January 9, 2024.
The PeRC agreed with granting a full waiver on the basis that TRYVIO fails to represent a

meaningful therapeutic benefit over existing therapies and is unlikely to be used in the pediatric
population.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With
the Investigational New Drug Application
13.1.1. Pharmacology
13.1.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
Evaluation of pharmacological activity of aprocitentan on endothelin (ET) receptors are
summarized in the table below. In in vitro studies using Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells
expressing human ETA or ETB receptors, aprocitentan showed antagonistic properties at both ET
receptor subtypes (ETA and ETB) in a competitive radioligand binding assay and inhibited the
ET-1-induced increase in cytosolic Ca2+ in a functional assay. In the isolated rat aorta and
trachea, aprocitentan inhibited ET-1 (ETA-mediated) and sarafotoxin S6c (ETB-mediated)
induced contractions, respectively. In the in vivo study, aprocitentan dose-dependently increased
ET-1 concentrations at doses ≥3 mg/kg in normotensive rats, suggesting that aprocitentan
blocked the binding of ET-1 to ETB receptors in normal rats.

TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Proof-of-concept for the efficacy of aprocitentan was assessed in in vivo animal models. As
summarized in the table below, various hypertension rat models were used to evaluate potential
effects of aprocitentan on hemodynamic parameters.
Aprocitentan showed acute antihypertensive effects, including decreased mean arterial pressure
(MAP), in salt-sensitive and volume-dependent hypertensive (i.e., deoxycorticosterone acetate
[DOCA]-salt rat and Dahl-S rat) or spontaneously hypertensive (SHR) rat models. The long-term
effect of aprocitentan on hemodynamics and potential for end organ damage was further assessed
in DOCA-salt rats. Results showed that aprocitentan improved renal blood flow and renal
vascular resistance, in addition to decreased MAP, and a tendency for improved left ventricular
weight, aorta and coronary artery thickness, and N-terminal pro-brain natriuretic peptide
(NT-proBNP) plasma concentration.

The effects of various treatment combinations with aprocitentan on blood pressure are
summarized in the table below. In DOCA-salt rats, coadministration of aprocitentan and
valsartan decreased MAP beyond a value predicted by an assumption of additive behavior,
demonstrating synergism between the two compounds. In contrast, spironolactone combined
with valsartan produced a purely additive effect. Coadministration of aprocitentan with enalapril
demonstrated synergy in SHR but simple additive effects in DOCA-salt rats. Aprocitentan and
amlodipine combination produced additive effects in SHR and DOCA-salt rats. Overall, these
combination studies demonstrated that the addition of aprocitentan may be anticipated to have an
additive to synergistic impact on blood pressure (BP) reduction when coadministered with other
antihypertensive drugs.

13.1.1.2. Secondary Pharmacology
In an off-target screening assessment (Study No: T-15.025), the potential for interaction with 53
known receptor systems or enzymes was assessed. Aprocitentan (10µM) did not produce any
noteworthy effects (defined as >50% inhibition or stimulation) in any of these assays.
13.1.1.3. Safety Pharmacology
A battery of safety pharmacology assessments (central nervous system, cardiovascular, and
respiratory endpoints) was conducted, and no significant safety concerns were identified.
Decreased BP and a compensatory increase in heart rate, which is considered related to the

Distribution
The volume of distribution (Vd) of aprocitentan was determined in rats (0.5 mg/kg, IV) (B05.130) and dogs (0.3 mg/kg, IV) (B-13.249). The mean Vdss was 1.0 L/kg in the rat and 0.3
L/kg in the dog, which approximates total body water volume.
The tissue distribution of aprocitentan (0.3 mg/kg, P.O, 14C-radiolabel) was evaluated in male
albino Wistar rats (for 10 days) and pigmented Lister Hooded rats (for 15 days) using
quantitative whole-body autoradiography (B-14.029). Absorption and distribution of
radioactivity into tissues were similar in both strains. The liver, kidney cortex, lung, pituitary,
thyroid, and myocardium showed the highest exposures, with peak exposures observed at 8
hours postdose. No relevant retention of radioactivity in the uveal tract/retina, pigmented skin, or
meninges of the brain was observed at any time point.
Binding of aprocitentan (14C-radiolabel, 0.1–300 μg/mL) to plasma proteins was measured in
mouse, rat, rabbit, dog, and human plasma using equilibrium dialysis (B-07.077). High plasma
protein binding (>98%) was observed in all species, with a free fraction between 0.1% to 0.5%
(Table 51).
Table 51. Plasma Protein Binding of Aprocitentan in Different Species

Source: Applicant-generated table (Table 6) from Pharmacokinetics Written Summary.

Partitioning into red blood cells was assessed in Study B-07.077. Mean blood/plasma ratios were
observed in the range of 0.54 to 0.69 for all species, indicating that aprocitentan has limited
ability to partition into red blood cells.
Metabolism
Metabolite structure was evaluated in vitro using rat and human hepatocytes, bile using bile ductcannulated rats, and in urine. Aprocitentan was heavily metabolized along five different
pathways, producing a total of 15 metabolites that were characterized, as shown in Figure 14.

All human metabolites were detected in the rat and/or the dog. No disproportionate human

In the repeated dose rat and dog toxicity studies, aprocitentan increased the mRNA expression of
cyp3a1, cyp2b1, cyp2b2, cyp2b2, ugt1a1, ugt1a6 and ugt2b1 (B-17.046) in rats and cyp2A12

induction in liver.

TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Excretion
The major excretion pathway for aprocitentan in rats was identified as biliary based on a study in
bile duct-cannulated rats. In this study, oral (3 mg) or intravenous (1 mg) administration of
aprocitentan (14C-labeled ACT-132577B) accounted for 50% of total oral dose and 70% of total
intravenous dose recovered in bile. Renal excretion represented 16% and 14% of total dose for
the oral and intravenous routes, respectively. Approximately 30% of the total oral dose and 22%
of total intravenous dose was recovered in feces (B-14.034). At 72 hours, the mean total recovery
was approximately 90% of the total drug exposure for the oral route and complete elimination of
the drug was measured following intravenous administration.
No milk excretion studies were conducted with aprocitentan; however, aprocitentan was
consistently observed in milk samples across all time points after oral administration of 14Clabeled macitentan (B-11.837 Annex) in rats.
Toxicokinetics
In repeat-dose oral studies, systemic exposure generally increased in a dose-dependent manner in
both rats and dogs. The exposure was decreased at the end of the dosing period in rats compared
to Day 1. The toxicokinetic parameters in the pivotal toxicology studies are summarized in the
table below.

13.1.3. Toxicology
13.1.3.1. General Toxicology
Repeat dose toxicology studies were conducted in rats (up to 26 weeks) and dogs (up to 39
weeks). These animal species were considered relevant for human risk assessment because
pharmacodynamic (PD) and PK/ADME profiles are similar to those in humans. Key results are
summarized in the following subsections.

dogs, whereas aprocitentan
(ACT-132577E) was administered in all rat general

13.1.3.1.1. Repeat-Dose Toxicity Studies in Rats
Repeat-dose toxicity studies were conducted in rats for up to 26-weeks with oral dosing of 10 to
1000 mg/kg/day aprocitentan. Two 26-week toxicity studies were conducted with the
(b) (4)
aprocitentan
form (10 to 250 mg/kg/day). The first study was conducted in Wistar rats. To
further evaluate the thyroidal follicular hyperplasia and adenomas observation in the first study, a
supplemental study was conducted only in male Wistar and Sprague Dawley (SD) rats. Key
findings from each 26-week study are summarized in the table below.
26-Week (9-Week Recovery) Toxicology Studies
Study Number/Title: 8310210/ACT-132577E: 26 Week Oral (Gavage) Administration
Toxicity Study in the Rat Followed by a 9 Week Treatment-Free Period
Key Study Finding
•

Dose dependent increases in the incidence and severity of dark and enlarged liver was
observed with an associated increase in organ weights due to hepatocellular hypertrophy.
These changes were due to reversible microsomal enzyme induction, considered an adaptive
response.

•

At the highest dose of 250 mg/kg/day, adverse focal to multifocal centrilobular hydropic
degeneration of hepatocytes were observed.

•

Reversible follicular hypertrophy at ≥10 mg/kg/day and hyperplasia at ≥50 mg/kg/day in the
thyroid glands were considered adaptive response as a consequence of increased hepatic
metabolism. Follicular adenomas observed at doses ≥100 mg/kg/day in this study was not
observed in the supplemental study at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day in male SD and Wistar rats
for the same duration. The findings in the thyroid were not considered clinically relevant.

•

The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was determined to be 50 mg/kg/day (AUC0-24
h values of 619 h*µg/mL, approximately 18-fold the AUC at the maximum recommended
human dose [MRHD]), based on clinically irrelevant thyroid follicular adenomas at
≥100 mg/kg/day and multifocal hydropic degeneration of hepatocytes at 250 mg/kg/day.

Table 55. Observations and Results for Study Number 8310210/ACT-132577E
Parameters
Major Findings
Mortality
One female animal in control group due to multiple clinical signs including abnormal
respiration, protruding eyes and weight loss, and one male animal in 250 mg/kg/day
group due to impaired mobility were sacrificed early. As no other animals showed a
similar clinical observation at 250 mg/kg/day, the impaired mobility was not
considered treatment related.
Clinical signs
Minor findings in the treatment groups were not considered adverse effects.
Body weights
Slight decrease in mean body weight gain was noted (-8.0% in male and -19% in
female) at 250 mg/kg/day and no differences were noted during the recovery phase.
Ophthalmoscopy No treatment-related adverse findings were observed.
Hematology
No treatment related changes were noted except a slight reduction in RBC, Hb and
PCV (-7%).
Clinical chemistry Dose-dependent increase in total protein in all the doses tested, with associated
increase in globulin ≥100 mg/kg/day was observed in female animals. Cholesterol
was increased in females at ≥100 mg/kg/day, and alanine aminotransferase was
increased in males at 250 mg/kg/day. These changes were reversed at the 9-week
recovery phase.
Gross pathology
Liver: Dose-dependent increase in dark and enlarged liver was noted at all the
doses tested with increased severity with dose. It was not recovered in two females
at 250 mg/kg/day.
Kidney: Dose-dependent increase in dark kidney was mainly observed in female
(except one male at 250 mg/kg/day) animals. It was not recovered in one female at
10 mg/kg/day and two females at 100 mg/kg/day and three females at
250 mg/kg/day.
Thyroid glands: Dose dependent enlarged lobes were observed in thyroids.
Organ weights
Reversible dose dependent increase in organ absolute and/or relative weights (body
weight and brain weight) were observed in liver (males ≥10 mg/kg/day and females
≥10 mg/kg/day), thyroid gland (≥100 mg/kg/day) and kidney (≥100 mg/kg/day).

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Parameters
Histopathology
Adequate battery:
Yes

Major Findings
Liver: Dose-dependent increased incidence and/or severity of hepatocellular
hypertrophy (centrilobular to diffuse, minimal to marked) was observed. These
changes were fully reversible after the recovery period and were likely related to
enzyme induction, hence, were considered adaptive response rather than adverse
effects. At 250 mg/kg/day, a focal-to-multifocal hydropic degeneration of
hepatocytes, mainly at a centrilobular location was observed, which was fully
resolved at the end of recovery. The affected hepatocytes appeared
swollen/ballooned with clear cytoplasm occasionally associated with increased
apoptosis.
Kidney: Brownish pigment was observed (males ≥50 mg/kg/day and females
≥10 mg/kg/day) in the tubular epithelia (localized mainly at proximal tubules in the
outer cortex) with more incidences in females and it is not fully reversed.
Thyroid glands: Follicular hypertrophy (males at ≥10 mg/kg/day and in females at
≥50 mg/kg/day) was observed. Follicular hyperplasia (distributed mainly diffusely,
rarely focally as nodular hyperplasia) was observed in males and females at
≥50 mg/kg/day. These reversible changes were considered adaptive response to
increased metabolism (increased hepatic glucuronosyltransferases (ugt1a1, 1a6,
2b1), which are responsible for metabolism of thyroid hormones) and excretion of
thyroid hormones and not considered significant test article related adverse effects.
Follicular adenomas were observed in two males at 100 mg/kg/day, three males,
and one female at 250 mg/kg/day. These lesions were partially resolved in the
recovery period.

Testes: No effect on testes was reported. Minor observations in 1 or 2 animals at
10 mg/kg/day and at 250 mg/kg/day were considered sporadic due to lack of any
dose responsiveness.
[Other evaluations] n/a

To further evaluate the occurrence of thyroidal follicular hyperplasia and adenomas in this 26week rat study, a supplemental 26-week study (T-16.021) was performed with a detailed thyroid
functional and histopathological evaluation in male Wistar and SD rats. This study is reviewed
below.
Study Number/Title: T-16.021/ACT-132577E – 26-Week Toxicity Study by the Oral Route
(Gavage) in the Male Rat Followed by a 9-Week Treatment-Free Period
Key Study Finding
Adverse histopathology findings:
•

A dose-dependent increase in centrilobular hepatocellular hypertrophy accompanied by
increased centrilobular hepatocellular fatty change was observed in liver. At 250 mg/kg/day a
moderate hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration/necrosis of hypertrophic hepatocytes was
observed.

•

Reversible increases in thyroid-stimulating hormone were noted in Wistar rats
(250 mg/kg/day) and SD rats (all the dose levels tested), establishing the cause of thyroid
follicular cell hyperplasia and adenomas observed in the original 26-week rat study.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
•

Reversible findings in the thyroid gland included a dose-dependent minimal to slight
follicular cell hypertrophy with correlating enlarged thyroid and increased weight at
≥50 mg/kg/day and minimal or slight focal follicular cell hyperplasia in two SD rats treated
with 250 mg/kg/day. No follicular adenomas were observed in this study.

•

Minimal dilation of seminiferous tubules (50 or 250 mg/kg/day) and increased incidence of
tubular degeneration/atrophy was observed in testes at 250 mg/kg/day which were resolved
completely at the end of recovery.

•

The NOAEL was determined to be 50 mg/kg/day, based on liver necrosis and testicular
tubular degeneration/atrophy observed at 250 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL exposures represented
AUC0-last values of 554 h*µg/mL in Wistar rats and 474 h*µg/mL in SD rats, or
approximately 16 and 14-fold the AUC at MRHD, respectively.

13.1.3.1.2. Repeat Dose Toxicity Studies in Dogs
Repeat dose toxicity studies were conducted in dogs for up to 39 weeks with oral dose levels
from 5 to 1000 mg/kg/day. Key findings are summarized below.
39-Week (13-Week Recovery) Toxicology Study
Study Number/Title: T-15.053/ACT-132577: 39 Week Oral (Capsule) Administration
Toxicity Study in the Dog Followed by a 13 Week Treatment-Free Period
Key Study Findings
•

Dose related occurrence of noisy respiration was observed at ≥25 mg/kg/day.

•

Clinical signs, including hunched posture, pale or thin appearance, and subdued/sluggish
behavior were observed at the high dose (75 mg/kg/day), leading to its reduction to
50 mg/kg/day starting from Day 163 in males and Day 165 in females.

•

Testes: minimal to slight tubular degeneration at doses ≥25 mg/kg/day.

•

Enlarged liver lobes and/or mottled livers were observed (males at 25 mg/kg/day and both
sexes at 75/50 mg/kg/day) with associated increased weights and hepatocellular hypertrophy
at ≥25 mg/kg/day. These changes were fully resolved at the end of recovery.

•

The NOAEL was determined at 5 mg/kg/day, based on noisy respiration, tubular
degeneration in the testes at higher doses. The NOAEL was associated with AUC0-24hr values
of 374 h*µg/mL, approximately 11-fold the exposure at MRHD.

13.1.3.3. Carcinogenicity Studies
No dedicated carcinogenicity studies were conducted with aprocitentan. Aprocitentan is a major
metabolite of macitentan, and its exposure represented ≥38% of the total exposure to macitentan
and aprocitentan in the macitentan carcinogenicity studies. Therefore, the macitentan
carcinogenicity studies, conducted in B6C3F1 mice and Wistar rats, were considered acceptable
to assess the carcinogenic potential of aprocitentan. The studies in mice and rats were conducted
in accordance with good laboratory practice (GLP) regulations and prior concurrence with the
CDER’s Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (CAC) was obtained regarding the
strain, number of animals employed, and doses evaluated. Both studies were reviewed previously
in NDA 204410 and were concluded to be negative, as described in the label of macitentan.
Below is a brief summary of the results of those studies based on previous review.
13.1.3.3.1. T-09.160: ACT-064992: 104-Week
Oncogenicity (Gavage) Study in the B6C3F1 Mouse
Male and female B6C3F1 mice (60 mice/sex/group for main study and 24 mice/sex/group for
toxicokinetic evaluation) were treated with macitentan (vehicle; 0.5% methylcellulose in purified
water, 5, 30, 100, and 400 mg/kg/day, oral) for a duration of 104 weeks, except for females of
the 400 mg/kg dose group. Due to high morbidity/mortality in females (35/60), the
400 mg/kg/day group was led to early termination at Week 79. The cause of death could not be
established in most animals.
Clinical signs of respiratory distress (e.g., breathing noises, labored breathing, tachypnea, and
cyanosis) were observed in males at 400 mg/kg/day and in females at ≥100 mg/kg/day from
Week 33 onwards. Dose-related decrease in body weights was observed in males (-4%, -7%,

124
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
and -17% final body weights at 30, 100, and 400 mg/kg/day, respectively) and in females (-8%
and -19% final body weights at 30 and 100 mg/kg/day, respectively) mice.
In histological evaluation, treatment-related proliferation of the nasal mucosa and submucosal
glands was observed with associated increased incidence of inflammatory infiltration in the
submucosa / submucosal glands. Hyaline inclusions and increased secretion were observed at
doses ≥100 mg/kg/day, which could be the reason for the respiratory distress noticed in this
study. The incidence of hepatocellular hypertrophy was increased in males at all dose levels.
Mean severity and incidence of endometrial cysts in the uterus was increased in females at
100 mg/kg/day, which was associated with increase in uterus organ weight.
In this study macitentan (ACT-064992) did not increase the incidence of neoplastic lesions when
tested up to 400 mg/kg/day in male mice and 100 mg/kg/day in female mice, which is a
maximum tolerated dose (MTD) in males and females, respectively. The aprocitentan exposure
(AUC) at macitentan dose of 100 mg/kg/day in Week 26 provided approximately 34-fold
(females) and 50-fold (males) human exposure at the MRHD of aprocitentan (12.5 mg).
Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee Conclusions
•

The Committee found that the study was acceptable, noting prior Executive CAC
concurrence with the protocol.

•

The Committee concurred that there were no drug-related neoplasms.
13.1.3.3.2. T-09.159: ACT-064992: 104-Week
Oncogenicity (Gavage) Study in the Wistar Rat

Male and female rats (Wistar, 51 rats/sex/group) were treated with macitentan (vehicle; 0.5%
methylcellulose in purified water, 10, 50 and 250 mg/kg/day, oral) for a duration of 104 weeks,
except for female rats of the 50 and 250 mg/kg dose group. In the female rats, due to clinical
signs at doses ≥50 mg/kg/day and mortality at 250 mg/kg/day, the mid and high dose groups
were suspended in weeks 50 and 51 and resumed in week 52 at doses 25 and 50 mg/kg/day.
Body weight was decreased in males at 250 mg/kg/day and females at 50/25 and
250/50 mg/kg/day. Respiratory distress (breathing noises, labored breathing, tachypnea, and
cyanosis) was observed in a dose-dependent manner at all doses tested with higher incidences in
the females.
In this study, macitentan (ACT-064992) treatment did not increase the incidence of neoplastic
lesions when tested up to an MTD. The aprocitentan exposure (AUC) at macitentan dose of
50 mg/kg/day dose in Week 26, provided approximately 8-fold (male) and 14-fold (females) the
human AUC at the MRHD for aprocitentan.
Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee Conclusions
•

The Committee found that the study was acceptable, noting prior Executive CAC
concurrence with the protocol.

•

The Committee concurred that there were no drug-related neoplasms.

13.1.3.4.2. Embryo-Fetal Development
EFD studies were cross-referenced from the NDA 204410 for Opsumit (macitentan) and the
results are summarized below. Aprocitentan is an active and the major metabolite of macitentan.
Its exposure in rats and rabbits represented ≥50% of total exposure with administration of
macitentan.
Rat Embryofetal Development Toxicity Study
A preliminary embryo-fetal toxicity study (Study # T-05.008) was conducted in Wistar rats for
the purpose of dose selection in the main embryo-fetal toxicity studies. Administration of
macitentan (0, 150, 450 and 1500 mg/kg/day) from Day 6 to Day 17 of the gestation period
resulted maternal toxicity at 1500 mg/kg/day and embryo-fetal toxicity at all doses tested.
Therefore, 150 mg/kg/day was selected as the highest dose in the main study. Below are the
definitive study results.
Study Number/Title: T-12.804/ACT-132577E: ACT-064992: Oral (Gavage) Embryo-Fetal
Development Study in the Rat With Toxicokinetic Sampling
Key Study Findings
•

Fetal abnormalities, defects in the head/neck, heart vessels and skeletal (skull), were
observed at all the doses tested.

•

Macitentan treatment was teratogenic in rats starting at the lowest dose tested (3 mg/kg/day),
which corresponds to an AUC0-24 h of 36.8 µg*hr/mL of aprocitentan. This is approximately
equivalent to the exposure at MRHD for aprocitentan based on AUC.

GLP Compliance: Yes

13.1.3.4.3. Rat Pre- and Postnatal Development Study
Study Number/Title: T-09.617/ACT-064992: Study for Effects on Pre- and Postnatal
Development Including Maternal Function in the Wistar Rat
The PPND study was cross-referenced from the macitentan application, NDA 204410, Opsumit,
and the results are summarized below.
Key Study Findings
F0 Dams
•

Postnatal loss was increased at all dose levels resulting in decreased birth and viability
indices.

•

Preweaning loss was increased with corresponding decrease in weaning index at
250 mg/kg/day.

•

Several pups were found dead during the first litter check after weaning and “no milk in the
stomach” was observed in several pups during the lactation period.

F1 Pups
•

Mean bodyweight and body weight gain were reduced on Day 21 and remained reduced on
Day 42.

•

Balano-preputial separation and opening of vagina were delayed at 250 mg/kg/day.

•

At necropsy the following effects were observed.
 Liver-enlarged hepatocytes at periportal region, bile duct hyperplasia and inflammatory
foci in the liver.
 Male reproductive organs – reduced size of testes and epididymides with correlated
reduced organ weights in some F1 pups at all treated dose levels; increased incidence of
minimal/slight tubular atrophy in F1 males at dam dose of 250 mg/kg/day.

F1 Generation
•

Number of female animals that did not mate or were not pregnant increased.

•

Preimplantation loss increased with reduced number of implantation sites and reduced mean
number of liver embryos at termination.

•

Liver size was reduced with thickened or thickened/enlarged lobes. Hepatocytes were
increased in size at periportal location, bile duct hyperplasia and some inflammatory foci
were observed in some animals of all treated groups.

•

Testes, epididymides and seminal vesicles reduced in size with correlated reduction in organ
weights. Increased incidence of minimal/slight tubular atrophy was noticed at
250 mg/kg/day.

Study Number/Title: T-22.030/ACT-132577E: ACT-132577E: An Embryo-Fetal
Development Study by Oral (Gavage)
Key Study Findings
•

Dose selection justification was insufficient (i.e., too low) to adequately characterize the
potential developmental toxicity of aprocitentan.

•

There were no treatment-related adverse effects at doses tested up to the highest dose of
10 mg/kg/day regarding maternal and developmental toxicity, which was associated with an
AUC0-24 h of 165000 ng*hr/mL for aprocitentan, approximately 5-fold the AUC at MRHD.

•

While the minor observations in fetal visceral and skeletal examinations could not be
attributed to aprocitentan treatment given similar observations reported in the control and
historical control groups, fetal observations were similar to those reported in studies with the
macitentan and other ERAs.

Justification for Dose Selection
A dose range-finding (DRF) study (Study No. T-22.029) was conducted for aprocitentan (0.3, 1,
3, 10 mg/kg/day) in rats. No mortality was observed in the DRF study. Reduction in food
consumption (-21% between Day 6-18 and -15.7% between Day 6-21) was correlated with lower
maternal body weight (4.3 to 9.2% at gestational day (GD) 6-9, 10 to 12.1% at GD 9-13, 6.89.7% on GD 7-17) at 10 mg/kg/day. Reduction in mean fetal body weights (7%, combined males
and females) was observed at 10 mg/kg/day. Other observations including minor clinical signs
(suspected dehydration, thin body appearance and hunched position) were not dose limiting.
Except a slight reduction of mean fetal body weight (7%) at 10 mg/kg/day, no other treatment
related effects were observed in ovarian and uterine examination and litter observations. No
treatment related malformations or variations were observed. The Applicant selected
10 mg/kg/day as the highest dose based on a decrease in maternal body weight. Given the critical
importance of identifying both a lowest-observed adverse effect level and a NOAEL for
embryofetal toxicity with aprocitentan in the rat, doses selection was inadequate.

TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
Justification for Dose Selection
A DRF study (Study # T-22.027) was conducted for aprocitentan (0.25, 0.75, 2.5 mg/kg/day) in
rabbits. The Applicant referred to a nonpregnant rabbit study in which maximum tolerability of
aprocitentan was tested. Based on the exposure (AUC0-24 h of 3740 µg*hr/mL) observed at
10 mg/kg/day in this nonpregnant rabbit study, a dose of 2.5 mg/kg/day was selected as the
highest dose for the DRF study with an aim to achieve one fourth of the exposure (AUC0-24 h of
935 µg*hr/mL). In the DRF study, no mortality was observed. No treatment-related effects on
body weight, food consumption, clinical signs, gravid uterine weights, maternal necropsy,
ovarian and uterine parameters and litter parameters, fetal examination parameters were observed
at doses tested, and an MTD could not be identified. The high dose tested achieved 29-fold the
clinical exposure at the MRHD of 12.5 mg/day, which may be considered appropriate, though a
lowest-observed adverse effect level for embryofetal toxicity could not be identified in the rabbit.

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding
Study B-07.077
B-07.077 examined the plasma protein binding and plasma/red blood cell partitioning of
aprocitentan in vitro using the 14C-labelled derivative ACT-132577B in a concentration range
from 0.1 to 300 µg/mL. The overall mean binding of ACT-132577B to plasma proteins over the
whole concentration range in humans was 99%, with a mean free fraction of 1%. There was no
obvious concentration-dependent effect in humans. Chromatogram radioactivity associated with
ACT-132577B in the plasma and buffer compartments after radio-high pressure liquid
chromatography showed that only about 90% of the plasma compartment radioactivity was
associated with ACT-132577B, and in the buffer compartment, there were two main peaks that
accounted for radioactivity. One peak corresponded to ACT-132577B and the other peak was an
unknown chemical. This substance was on the radiochromatogram of ACT-132577 (in the
starting material). The Applicant therefore believes that the compound in the dialysate is the
result of a selective transfer during the equilibrium dialysis experiment and not a postexperimental degradation product. Therefore, the protein binding values were corrected based on
the actual amount of ACT-132577. The corrected plasma protein binding was 99.5%, with a
mean corrected free fraction of 0.5%. Plasma/red blood cell partitioning was determined at
500 ng/mL and 100 µg/mL. The mean association of radioactivity with red blood cells at both
concentrations was 4.8%. The blood/plasma ratio was 0.54. This indicates that there was not a
significant uptake of radioactivity into red blood cells.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)

14.1.2. Metabolism Studies
Study B-14.033
B-14.033 looked at the metabolic profile of aprocitentan in human liver preparations. Six
metabolites were formed along two primary pathways involving hydrolysis to M1 and
glucosidation to M3.
Incubation of radiolabeled aprocitentan, ACT-132577B, with human liver microsomes led to
formation of M1 and M2 (4.3% and 2.1% of total chromatogram radioactivity, respectively). M1
was also observed in experiments without microsomes or a nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide
phosphate (NADPH)-regenerating system and was present at 9.3% and 6.4% total chromatogram
radioactivity, respectively, in comparable conditions.
Incubation of ACT-132577B with hepatocytes generated metabolites M1-M6. No metabolite
contributed to more than 5% total chromatogram radioactivity, and total turnover was 21%. Out
of 3 batches, M1-M6 was seen in batch 1, and M1, M2, and M5 were seen in batches 2 or 3. M1
was seen in the control experiment without liver cells and accounted for 8.7% total
chromatogram radioactivity after a 24-hour incubation.
Formation of M1 results from both chemical and enzymatic hydrolysis. M1 is then metabolized
to secondary metabolites M2 and M5 through P450/FMO-dependent reactions. M2 undergoes
further glucuronidation to M4, and M5 is oxidized to M6 in human hepatocytes.
Study B-14.050
This study determined the structure of aprocitentan metabolites with liquid chromatography
combined with high resolution mass spectrometry. Specifically, structure determination of
metabolites was performed from samples from the in vitro metabolite profiling study (B-14.033).
Metabolites from rat hepatocytes were also characterized. The proposed metabolic pathways of
aprocitentan are presented in Figure 16. In total, it appears that aprocitentan is metabolized along
5 metabolic pathways – hydrolysis to M1, glucosidation to M3, glucuronidation to M8, oxidative
debromination then glucuronidation to M10, and N-oxidation at the pyrimidine sulfamide moiety
to M9. Metabolite M9 would then subsequently undergo glucuronidation to M22. M1 came from
hydrolysis of the sulfamide moiety to an aminopyrimidine and has been shown to be a product of
nonenzymatic hydrolysis and a true metabolite (See Study B-05.130 in Section 14.1.4).
Metabolites M9 and M26 undergo glucuronidation to metabolites M22 and M10, respectively.
Additional downstream metabolites of M1 include M2, M11, M4, M21, M15, M5, M13, M6, and
M16.

Study B-18.023
B-18.023 was an in vitro study conducted to identify the human uridine diphosphateglucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzymes involved in the formation of the aprocitentan glucoside
M3 metabolite. 14C-lablelled aprocitentan was incubated with recombinant human UGT
enzymes. UGT enzyme activity is listed in Table 76. Aprocitentan was metabolized to M3 by
UGT1A1 and UGT2B7. M3 formation was 1.1% for UGT1A1 and 1.4% for UGT2B7 after 60
mins, indicating turnover was low. Additionally, the chemical hydrolysis product M1 was the
most prevalent entity observed in UGT experiments and control incubations without UDPG or
UGT enzymes. M1 was similar in all samples ranging from 20 to 23% after 60 mins.

14.1.3. Transporter Characterization
Study B-17.068
B-17.068 is an in vitro drug transporter substrate and permeability study to determine if
aprocitentan is a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP)
and to investigate the in vitro permeability of aprocitentan.
Efflux ratios of aprocitentan in MDCKII-MDR1 cells were 2.3 to 3.5 and decreased to 0.8-0.9 in
the presence of zosuquidar, a selective P-gp inhibitor. In a few cases, the recoveries were below
70%, but the Applicant considers these samples valid since the Papp values were in agreement
with the rest of the values. The data indicates that aprocitentan is a substrate of P-gp.
Efflux ratios of aprocitentan in MDCKII-BCRP cells were 7.3 to 23.2 and decreased to 0.8 to 1.3
in the presence of Ko143, a BCRP inhibitor. The data indicates that aprocitentan is a substrate of
BCRP. In a few cases, the recoveries were below 70%, but the Applicant considers these samples
valid since the Papp values were in agreement with the rest of the values. Overall, the data
indicates that aprocitentan is a substrate of BCRP.

143
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5347932

NDA 217686
TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
The apparent absorptive permeability coefficients of aprocitentan in MDCKII cells were 13.6 x
10-6 cm/s, indicating moderate to high permeability.
Study B-10.642
Study B-10.642 evaluated if aprocitentan is a substrate of human hepatic organic anion
transporting polypeptides (OATPs) OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OATP2B1. Uptake of
aprocitentan into wild-type and OATP-expressing CHO cells was high and rapid. There was no
apparent difference in the uptake of aprocitentan between wild-type and OATP1B1 cells. The
uptake of aprocitentan into OATP1B3 and OATP2B1 cells at concentrations less than 4µM was
maximally 40% higher than into wild-type cells. No marked difference in the rate of uptake into
wild-type and OATP1B1 cells was observed over 0.01 to 300µM. The uptake ratios for
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OATP2B1 were a maximum of 1.1, 1.3, and 1.4, respectively, with
incubation times ranging from 0 to 60 seconds, and concentrations ranging from 1µM to 3µM.
The positive control of estrone-3-sulphate had an uptake ratio of 4.5, 5.6, and 11.6 for
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and OATP2B1, respectively, at 5 minutes at 5µM. The data indicate that
cellular uptake of aprocitentan does not depend on human OATP transporters but is driven by
passive diffusion, though there may be some contribution from active uptake.
Additionally, aprocitentan inhibited OATP1B1-mediated uptake of atorvastatin and OATP1B3and OATP2B1-mediated uptake of estrone-3-sulphase. The mean half maximal inhibitory
concentration (IC50) values for aprocitentan were 21, 56, and 15µM for OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
and OATP2B1, respectively.

14.1.4. Drug-Drug Interactions
Study B-05.044
B-05.044 studied the effect of ACT-064992 (macitentan) and ACT-132577 (aprocitentan) on bile
salt transport. Specifically, the potential to inhibit sodium-dependent taurocholate
co-transporting polypeptide (NTCP) and human bile salt export pump (BSEP) was investigated.
NTCP inhibition was tested in CHO F1p In™ cells over-expressing NTCP. BSEP inhibition was
tested in Sf9 membrane vesicles over-expressing BSEP.
Aprocitentan inhibited NTCP- and BSEP-mediated uptake of taurocholic acid, with a mean IC50
value of 14µM and 50µM, respectively.
Study B-05.130
B-05.130 studied a panel of cytochrome p450 (CYP) inhibition assays to assess the potential of
drug-drug interactions with concomitantly administered CYP450 substrates. Specifically, the
potential to inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, and
CYP3A4 was investigated. See Table 77.
